T\big[{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(1/2)}{\cal L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1/2)}\big]

•

Lagrangian insertions involving the leading power hard scattering operator, through the time-ordered products
T\big[{\cal L}_{\mathrm{hard}}^{(0)}{\cal L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1/2)}{\cal L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1/2)}\big]
and
T\big[{\cal L}_{\mathrm{hard}}^{(0)}{\cal L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1)}\big]

Except for the kinematic corrections, all sources of power corrections are related to the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
Lagrangians at subleading power.
All four of these sources for power corrections will be analyzed in
section
4
, and final results for the NLP
W_{i}
s will be given in
section
5
.

4
SIDIS Factorization to Next-to-Leading Power (NLP)

In this section, we derive the factorization formula for SIDIS in the limit of small transverse momentum.
More precisely, we study the hadronic structure functions
W_{i}

that enter the angular decomposition of the SIDIS cross section in eqs. (
2.24
) and (
2.34
).
The
W_{i}=P_{i}^{\mu\nu}W_{\mu\nu}
are projections of the hadronic tensor
W^{\mu\nu}

onto the basis of projectors defined in eq. (
2.2.1
), and thus studying their factorization
is equivalent to factorizing
W^{\mu\nu}
itself. The advantage of considering the
W_{i}

is that it takes into account power corrections from the projectors themselves,
thereby yielding the power expansion of the SIDIS cross section.

Our derivation is based on SCET, reviewed in
section
3
, and follows the procedure of ref.
[
115
]
for the treatment of label and residual momentum in the multipole expansion.
We first define a symbolic power counting parameter

\displaystyle\lambda\sim P_{hT}/Q\,,

(4.1)

in terms of which we can expand the structure functions as

\displaystyle W_{i}

\displaystyle=W_{i}^{(0)}+W_{i}^{(1)}+\cdots\,.

(4.2)

Leading-power (LP) contributions
W_{i}^{(0)}
scale as
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-2})
,
while next-to-leading power (NLP) contributions
W_{i}^{(1)}
have a relative suppression by one power of
\lambda

and thus scale as
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-1})
, and so on.
We will only consider structure functions up to NLP,
as starting at NNLP most structure functions receive corrections from subleading Lagrangian contributions
that are beyond the scope of this paper.

While the power expansion of the projectors
P_{i}^{\mu\nu}
is straightforward,
the factorization of the hadronic tensor is highly nontrivial.
Recall its definition in eq. (
2.1
),

\displaystyle W^{\mu\nu}(q,P_{N},P_{h})

\displaystyle=\SumInt_{X}\,\delta^{4}(q+P_{N}-P_{h}-P_{X})\,\braket{N|J^{\dagger\,\mu}(0)|h,X}\braket{h,X|J^{\nu}(0)|N}

\displaystyle=\SumInt_{X}\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}b}{(2\pi)^{4}}\,e^{\mathrm{i}b\cdot q}\braket{N|J^{\dagger\,\mu}(b)|h,X}\braket{h,X|J^{\nu}(0)|N}\,,

where for the moment we neglect polarizations of the target nucleon
N
.
In eq. (
4
), we abbreviate the sum over all states
X
and the corresponding phase space integral by
\SumInt_{X}
,
and in the second line used momentum conservation to shift the position of the first current.

While eq. (
4
) is manifestly Lorentz covariant, explicit expressions do depend on the choice of frame
in which the factorization it is discussed. From now on, we will always neglect hadron masses
and work in the factorization frame characterized by eq. (
2.42
),

\displaystyle P_{N}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{Q}{x}\frac{n^{\mu}}{2}\,,\qquad P_{h}^{\mu}=zQ\frac{{\bar{n}}^{\mu}}{2}\,,

(4.19)

where
n^{\mu}
and
{\bar{n}}^{\mu}
are lightlike reference vectors. This
n
and
{\bar{n}}
define the reference vectors for our two collinear SCET sectors in this back-to-back frame. In addition we have a soft sector as discussed in
section
3
.
For most of our results, the precise form of
P_{N}^{-}
and
P_{h}^{+}
in eq. (
4.19
)
will not matter, but occasionally we will explicitly make use of this particular choice to simplify results.

As already mentioned in the introduction, in this paper we make the assumption that Glauber interactions from the SCET Lagrangian
{\cal L}_{G}^{(0)}
do not spoil factorization at NLP. We then work out the full form that the factorization formula must take for the set of
W_{i}
s which themselves start off at NLP.

The organization of our analysis below is as follows.
We start in
section
4.1
by discuss the hard scattering operators needed to NLP in
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
,
with the general setup in
section
4.1.1
, operators induced by integrating out hard interactions
in sections
4.1.2
–
4.1.5
,
and from integrating out hard-collinear interactions using
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
as an intermediate theory in
section
4.1.5
.
In
section
4.2
we review LP factorization using SCET, keeping track of the NLP contributions that come from implementing momentum conservation.
In
section
4.3
we discuss kinematic power corrections that enter from subleading contributions to the projectors
P_{i}^{\mu\nu}
(associated to differences between the Trento and factorization frames).
In
section
4.4
we show that contributions from
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
Lagrangian insertions vanish for SIDIS structure functions that start at NLP.
In
section
4.5
we demonstrate that soft contributions involving derivatives
n\cdot i\partial_{s}
,
{\bar{n}}\cdot i\partial_{s}
or the soft gluon building blocks
{\bar{n}}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}^{(n)}
,
n\cdot{\cal B}_{s}^{({\bar{n}})}
, and
\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})\mu}
vanish for all
W_{i}
at NLP,
and also discuss the vanishing at NLP of operators induced by the hard-collinear region of momentum space.
Finally, we discuss the nonvanishing contributions from operators including
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\perp}
insertions
in
section
4.6
and
section
4.7
, respectively.

4.1
Hard Operators in SCET

4.1.1
General Setup

The currents
J^{\mu}
in eq. (
4
) are the currents in full QCD. In the case considered here,
only the vector current
J_{{\bar{q}}q}^{\mu}=\bar{q}\gamma^{\mu}q
contributes, see eq. (
2.4
).
This current has to be matched onto the corresponding current in
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
,
which contains operators built from soft and collinear quark and gluon fields and the corresponding Wilson coefficients.
Schematically, this reads

\displaystyle J_{{\bar{q}}q}^{\mu}(x)=\sum_{\mathcal{O}}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}J_{\mathcal{O}}^{(j)\,\mu}\,,

(4.20)

where we sum over all relevant SCET operators
\mathcal{O}
, and where
J_{\mathcal{O}}^{(j)}

denotes the corresponding current at
\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{j})
in the power expansion.

The current in eq. (
4.20
) couples to the corresponding
leptonic vector current, see eq. (
2.3
). Since we work at tree level
in the electroweak theory, the leptonic current does not receive any corrections
and thus has trivial matching in the effective field theory (EFT).
This also implies that the photon field is not dynamic, and formally can be integrated out of the theory.
Thus, rather than performing the matching onto SCET at the level of the QCD current
as illustrated in eq. (
4.20
), we can equivalently consider the hard scattering Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm hard}=\frac{\mathrm{i}e^{2}}{Q^{2}}\,J_{\bar{\ell}\ell\,\mu}\sum_{q}J_{{\bar{q}}q}^{\mu}\,,

(4.21)

whose power expansion follows from eq. (
4.20
) as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{hard}}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{hard}}^{(j)}\,,\qquad\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{hard}}^{(j)}=\frac{\mathrm{i}e^{2}}{Q^{2}}\,\sum_{\mathcal{O}}J_{\bar{\ell}\ell\,\mu}J_{\mathcal{O}}^{(j)\,\mu}\,.

(4.22)

Note that matrix elements like
\langle\mathrm{i}{\cal L}_{\rm hard}\rangle
give rise to the amplitude, and the
e^{2}/Q^{2}
prefactors are from the virtual photon exchange as in eq. (
2.3
). The conjugate amplitudes are instead obtained from the matrix elements
\langle-\mathrm{i}{\cal L}_{\rm hard}^{\dagger}\rangle
. We do not impose the restriction of hermiticity on
{\cal L}_{\rm hard}
in this setup.
9
9

9

The fact that
{\cal L}_{\rm hard}^{\dagger}\neq{\cal L}_{\rm hard}
is clear from the example of leading power TMDs in DY where
C^{(0)}(\omega_{1}\omega_{2})
in eq. (
3.8
) is complex even though the operator is related by hermitian conjugation together with flipping
n_{1}\leftrightarrow n_{2}
and
\omega_{1}\to\omega_{2}
. These flips do not modify the Wilson coefficient.

This approach has several advantages.
First, the contraction with the leptonic current eliminates some contributions to
J_{{\bar{q}}q}^{\mu}
,
and thus facilitates the construction of a minimal basis for the hard matching.
Second, higher-order QCD corrections to the required amplitudes are typically
evaluated using the spinor-helicity formalism which is more naturally used with
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{hard}}
.
For this reason, this approach was previously used both at LP and NLP
[
107
,
71
]
.

The power-suppressed hard Lagrangians receive contributions which from eq. (
3.17
) can be divided into two categories:
\mathcal{L}_{\rm h}^{(i)}
whose Wilson coefficients contain contributions from the hard scale
\sim Q^{2}
, and
\mathcal{L}_{\rm hc}^{(j)}
whose Wilson coefficients also get contributions from the hard-collinear scale. The decomposition in eq. (
4.22
) applies equally well to both of these contributions, so they both can be discussed in the language of hadronic currents.
In the sections below we will first focus on enumerating all
\mathcal{L}_{\rm h}^{(j)}
contributions, before turning in
section
4.1.5
to
\mathcal{L}_{\rm hc}^{(j)}
contributions. It is important to note that at leading power there is only a
\mathcal{L}_{\rm h}^{(0)}
contribution, whereas the hard-collinear contributions start with
\mathcal{L}_{\rm hc}^{(j\geq 1/2)}
.
For
{\cal L}_{\rm h}^{(j)}
we can make eq. (
4.22
) more explicit by factoring out the hard
p^{2}\sim Q^{2}
flucutuations into Wilson coefficients
C^{(j)}
with convolutions involving
{\cal O}(\lambda^{0})
momenta
[
35
]
, by writing

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm h}^{(j)}(0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{n_{i}\}}\sum_{\{\lambda_{i}\}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}\omega_{n}\,

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathcal{O}^{A_{1}\cdots a_{n}\,(j)}_{\{\lambda_{i}\}}\bigl(\{n_{i}\};\{\omega_{i}\}\bigr)\,C_{\{\lambda_{i}\}}^{A_{1}\cdots a_{n}\,(j)}\bigl(\{n_{i}\};\{\omega_{i}\}\bigr)\,.

(4.23)

Here the Lagrangian is taken at position
b=0
since this is what we will need when making use of the hard scattering currents (and we can easily translate to include the full position space, respecting the SCET multipole expansion). The operators
are built from fields with fixed label momenta

\displaystyle\tilde{p}_{i}^{\mu}=\omega_{i}\frac{n_{i}^{\mu}}{2}\,.

(4.24)

In eq. (
4.1.1
), the first sum runs over all possible collinear reference directions
\{n_{i}\}
,
which will be fixed by the hard process in consideration.
The second sum runs over all operators contributing at this order,
which are labeled by the set of parameters
\{\lambda_{i}\}
.
For example, if one organizes the operators by particle helicities,
the labels would correspond to the individual helicities.
For an operator consisting of
n
fields (counting the lepton fields as well),
the
\omega_{1}\cdots\omega_{n}
are the label momenta of the fields, and are integrated over.
The operators
\mathcal{O}^{A_{1}\cdots a_{n}\,(j)}_{\{\lambda_{i}\}}

and Wilson coefficients
C_{\{\lambda_{i}\}}^{A_{1}\cdots a_{n}\,(j)}

are correspondingly labeled by the
\{\lambda_{i}\}
, and depend on both
the reference directions
\{n_{i}\}
and the label momenta
\{\omega_{i}\}
.
The superscripts denote the color index of the
n
particles,
with
A_{i},a_{i}
and
\bar{a}_{i}
denoting adjoint, fundamental
and antifundamental colors, and summation over colors is implied.
With respect to color, eq. (
4.1.1
) can be interpreted as having distinct
Wilson coefficients for each color configuration.

For more complicated operators involving multiple colored particles,
the color structure in eq. (
4.1.1
) becomes quite involved, and it is convenient
to express operators and Wilson coefficients as vectors in color space,
see refs.
[
107
,
71
]
for a discussion in the context of SCET matching.
In our case, the color space will be trivial.
At LP, the hard scattering operator is built from
q^{a}
and
\bar{q}^{\bar{b}}
fields,
and thus the unique color structure is

\displaystyle\hat{T}^{a\bar{b}}=\bigl(\delta_{a\bar{b}}\bigr)\,.

(4.25)

At NLP, we will at most have operators built from
g^{A}
,
q^{a}
and
\bar{q}^{\bar{b}}
fields,
which is still described by a unique color structure,

\displaystyle\hat{T}^{A\,a\bar{b}}=\bigl(T^{A}_{a\bar{b}}\bigr)\,.

(4.26)

Due to this simplicity, we refrain from using more advanced treatments of the appearing color structures,
but note that starting at NNLP operators with four colored fields arise,
in which case a nontrivial color algebra becomes necessary.

4.1.2
Leading Power

At leading power, the only hard operator is
[
115
]

\mathcal{O}_{f}^{(0)\alpha\beta}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2})=\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},-\omega_{2}}^{f\alpha\bar{a}}\,\left[S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\right]^{a\bar{b}}\chi_{{n_{1}},-\omega_{1}}^{f\beta b}\,,

(4.27)

where we sum over the spin indices
\alpha
and
\beta
and the color indices
\bar{a}
,
b
.
They depend on the light-cone directions
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
, which so far are still arbitrary,
and the corresponding light-cone momenta

{\tilde{p}}_{1}^{\mu}=\omega_{1}\frac{n_{1}^{\mu}}{2}\,,\qquad{\tilde{p}}_{2}^{\mu}=\omega_{2}\frac{{n}_{2}^{\mu}}{2}\,.

(4.28)

Using the notation in eq. (
4.22
), the leading-power hard current corresponding to eq. (
4.27
) is given by

J^{(0)\,\mu}(0)=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,\sum_{f}C_{f}^{(0)\,\mu\,\alpha\beta}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2})\,\mathcal{O}_{f}^{(0)\alpha\beta}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2})\,.

(4.29)

Here, the sum runs over all possible light-cone directions, which will be fixed once acting with this operator on specific states,
as well as the flavor
f
of the quark initiating the hard scattering.
The Wilson coefficient
C_{f}^{(0)\mu\,\alpha\beta}
in eq. (
4.29
) is written as

\displaystyle C_{f}^{(0)\,\mu\,\alpha\beta}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2})

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu})^{\alpha\beta}\,C_{f}^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu})^{\alpha\beta}\,\Bigl[Q_{f}C_{q}^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})+\sum_{f^{\prime}}Q_{f^{\prime}}C_{vf^{\prime}}^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})\Bigr]\,.

(4.30)

Here, we made explicit that by Lorentz invariance the right-hand side can only depend on
\tilde{q}^{2}=({\tilde{p}}_{1}+{\tilde{p}}_{2})^{2}=2{\tilde{p}}_{1}\cdot{\tilde{p}}_{2}=\omega_{1}\omega_{2}\,(n_{1}\cdot n_{2})/2
.
At LP we have the Dirac structure
\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu}
, which is orthogonal to both
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
, so
\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu}=\gamma^{\mu}-(n_{1}^{\mu}\not{n}_{2}+n_{2}^{\mu}\not{n}_{1})/(n_{1}\cdot n_{2})
.
In eq. (
4.1.2
),
C_{vf^{\prime}}^{(0)}
encodes contributions to the quark form factor from closed quark loops,
such that the quark coupling to the virtual photon is not identical to the quark extracted from the proton.
It first contributes at three loops,
C_{vf^{\prime}}^{(0)}=\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{3})
.

From now on, we will mostly leave implicit the quark flavor
f
superscript on the collinear quark fields using
\chi_{n}
in place of
\chi_{n}^{f}
, and do the same for Wilson coefficients and operators, eg. writing
C^{(0)}
instead of
C_{f}^{(0)}
. We will also suppress color indices when the contractions are clear.

We also define the conjugate operator and Wilson coefficient such that the usual factor of
\gamma^{0}
are included in the latter,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{(0)\,\dagger\beta\alpha}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2})

\displaystyle=\bar{\chi}_{{n}_{1},\omega_{1}}^{\beta}\,S_{n_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{2}}\,\chi_{n_{2},\omega_{2}}^{\alpha}=\mathcal{O}^{(0)\beta\alpha}(-{\tilde{p}}_{2},-{\tilde{p}}_{1})\,,

\displaystyle\bar{C}_{\mu}^{(0)\,\mu\,\beta\alpha}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2})

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\gamma^{0}C^{(0)\,\mu\,\dagger}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2})\gamma^{0}\Bigr]^{\beta\alpha}=(\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu})^{\beta\alpha}\,C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})\,.

(4.31)

The Wilson coefficient
C^{(0)}
in the
\overline{\text{MS}}
scheme can be calculated from the quark form factor in pure dimensional regularization. For the space-like case
\tilde{q}^{2}<0
that is relevant here, both the form factor and
C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})
are real, explaining why there is no need for a complex conjugation in eq. (
4.1.2
).

4.1.3
NLP Operators involving
{\cal P}_{\perp}
,
\partial_{s}
,
{\cal B}_{s}^{(n)}
,
{\cal B}_{s}^{({\bar{n}})}
and RPI Constraints

We now extend our discussion of hard scattering operators to subleading power for
{\cal L}_{h}^{(1)}
,
i.e. operators suppressed by
\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
that appear after integrating out hard scale fluctuations. Our analysis is based on ref.
[
71
]
,
which derived the complete set of subleading operators using the spinor-helicity formalism. The desired NLP
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
operators that appear at
{\cal O}(\lambda)
involve the operator structures:

\displaystyle\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{2}}\,,\quad\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}{\cal B}_{n_{i}\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{2}}\,,\quad\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}\mathrm{i}n_{1}\cdot\partial_{s}\chi_{n_{2}}\,,\quad\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}\mathrm{i}n_{2}\cdot\partial_{s}\chi_{n_{2}}\,,\quad\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}{\cal B}_{s}^{(n_{i})\mu}\chi_{n_{2}}\,.

(4.32)

In this section we will obtain the operators that involve
{\cal P}_{\perp}^{\mu}
,
n_{1}\cdot\partial_{s}
,
n_{2}\cdot\partial_{s}
,
{\bar{n}}_{2}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}^{(n_{1})}
, and
{\bar{n}}_{1}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}^{(n_{2})}
, for which it turns out that the Wilson coefficients are related to the leading power Wilson coefficient
C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})
in eq. (
4.1.2
).
There is also a partial constraint on the operator
{\cal B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})\mu}
discussed here, and this operator will be taken up in full in
section
4.1.5
. Finally, there is no constraint on operators involving

{\cal B}_{n_{1}\perp}^{\mu}
, which will be taken up independently in
section
4.1.4
below.

We start by considering the analog hard scattering operators in
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
, and constraints on the Wilson coefficients of these operators from the reparameterization invariance (RPI) symmetry of SCET
[
54
]
. The results for RPI relation for subleading power
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
dijet operators are available in the literature, see eg. refs.
[
111
,
65
,
71
]
, and we will review results we need here.
Performing the matching of
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}

\to

{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
we then obtain the analogous constraints on operators for
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
, since the hard Wilson coefficients for operators in
{\cal L}_{\rm h}^{(1)}
are not changed by this matching.

Reparameterization invariance (RPI) symmetry in SCET arises because of two freedoms in the construction: i) the freedom of how to divide up hierarchically large and small momentum components, and ii) the freedom to modify the choice of the reference vectors
n
and
{\bar{n}}
for each collinear sector
[
54
]
. This RPI symmetry relates the properties of operators that appear at different orders in the power expansion. In
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
, freedom i) connects operators with collinear and ultrasoft derivatives. When combined with gauge symmetry it can be implemented by the replacements

\displaystyle W_{n}^{\dagger}\mathrm{i}D_{n\perp}^{\mu}W_{n}

\displaystyle\to W_{n}^{\dagger}\mathrm{i}{\cal D}_{n\perp}^{\mu}W_{n}\equiv W_{n}^{\dagger}\mathrm{i}D_{n\perp}^{\mu}W_{n}+\mathrm{i}D_{{\rm us}\perp}^{\mu}\,,

(4.33)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{n}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{i}{\bar{n}}\cdot\partial_{n}^{\rm RPI}\equiv\bar{\mathcal{P}}_{n}+i{\bar{n}}\cdot D_{\rm us}\,,

in collinear operators, with a subsequent expansion in
\lambda
giving operators with the ultrasoft derivatives that are constrained to have the same hard Wilson coefficients (unless there is more than one source for the specific operator with ultrasoft fields). To implement constraints from ii) there are two possible equivalent methods, working out the RPI transformations on a basis of operators and then constructing linear combinations of operators that are invariant as in ref.
[
54
]
, or constructing RPI-invariant operators and then expanding them in
\lambda
as in ref.
[
111
]
. We will use this second approach here.

The RPI and gauge invariant operator whose power expansion yields the field structure of the leading power
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
hard scattering operator in eq. (
3.12
) is given by

\displaystyle C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})\bigl(\bar{\psi}_{n_{2}}\mathcal{W}_{n_{2}}\bigr)\delta\bigl(\omega_{2}-\mathrm{i}\,\bar{n}_{1}\!\cdot\!\overleftarrow{\partial}_{n_{2}}^{\rm RPI}\bigr)\gamma_{\mu}\,\delta(\omega_{1}+\mathrm{i}\,\bar{n}_{1}\!\cdot\partial_{n_{1}}^{\rm RPI}\bigr)\bigl(\mathcal{W}_{n_{1}}^{\dagger}\psi_{n_{1}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad=C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})\,(\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},-\omega_{2}})\gamma^{\perp}_{\mu}(\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}})+\ldots\,,

(4.34)

where
\tilde{q}^{2}=\omega_{1}\,\omega_{2}\,n_{1}\cdot n_{2}/2
,
\psi_{n}
is an RPI-invariant fermion field,
{\cal W}_{n}
is an RPI invariant Wilson line, and the
\delta
-functions have been made invariant under the RPI of type i). We can also consider additional terms to make the
\delta
-functions invariant under type ii) RPI, as done for example in Ref.
[
65
]
, but the additional terms do not play a role for the relations between
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
operators that we derive here. The ellipses in eq. (
4.1.3
) denote power suppressed terms that may be connected by RPI to this leading power operator, and thus be constrained to depend on the same Wilson coefficient
C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})
.

To obtain the terms needed for the translation to constraints in
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
we should expand the operator in eq. (
4.1.3
) to
{\cal O}(\lambda)
for subleading collinear terms, and to
{\cal O}(\lambda^{2})
for subleading ultrasoft terms.
The field
\psi_{n_{i}}
has the expansion
[
111
]

\displaystyle\psi_{n_{i}}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot D}\mathrm{i}\not{\cal D}_{n_{i}\perp}\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}_{i}}{2}\right)\xi_{n_{i}}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3})

\displaystyle=\xi_{n_{i}}+W_{n_{i}}\frac{1}{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{i}}}\left(\not{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{i}\perp}+g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{n_{i}\perp}+\mathrm{i}\not{D}_{{\rm us}\perp}\right)\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}_{i}}{2}W_{n_{i}}^{\dagger}\xi_{n_{i}}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{3})\,.

(4.35)

The RPI version collinear Wilson line is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{n_{i}}=W_{n_{i}}e^{-\mathrm{i}R_{n_{i}}}=W_{n_{i}}\big(1-\mathrm{i}R_{n_{i}}^{(1)}+\ldots\big)\,,

(4.36)

where
R_{n_{i}}^{(1)}\sim{\cal O}(\lambda)
and full expressions for the expansion are given in ref.
[
111
]
. We note that in terms of field structures,
R_{n_{i}}^{(1)}
contains a
(1/\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{i}})\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\perp}
.
Finally, we need to expand the
\delta
-functions in eq. (
4.1.3
), which gives

\displaystyle\delta(\omega_{i}+\mathrm{i}\,\bar{n}_{i}\!\cdot\partial_{n_{i}}^{\rm RPI}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(1+\mathrm{i}{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot D_{us}\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega_{i}}\Bigr)\delta(\omega_{i}+\mathrm{i}{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot\partial_{n_{i}})+{\cal O}(\lambda^{4})\,.

(4.37)

Here the
\partial/\partial\omega_{i}
derivative can be integrated by parts onto the Wilson coefficient.
Combining these expansions together to determine the ellipses in eq. (
4.1.3
), and making the BPS field redefinition, we find that the terms to the order we need are

\displaystyle C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})\bigl(\bar{\psi}_{n_{2}}\mathcal{W}_{n_{2}}\bigr)\delta\bigl(\omega_{2}-\mathrm{i}\,\bar{n}_{1}\!\cdot\!\overleftarrow{\partial}_{n_{2}}^{\rm RPI}\bigr)\gamma_{\mu}\,\delta(\omega_{1}+\mathrm{i}\,\bar{n}_{1}\!\cdot\partial_{n_{1}}^{\rm RPI}\bigr)\bigl(\mathcal{W}_{n_{1}}^{\dagger}\psi_{n_{1}}\bigr)

(4.38)

\displaystyle\quad=C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})\,(\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},-\omega_{2}})\big(1-\mathrm{i}R_{n_{2}}^{(1)}\big)(Y_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}Y_{n_{1}})\gamma^{\perp}_{\mu}\big(1+\mathrm{i}R_{n_{1}}^{(1)\dagger}\big)(\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}})

\displaystyle\quad+C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})\,\bigg\{(\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},-\omega_{2}})(Y_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}Y_{n_{1}})\bigg[\gamma^{\mu}\frac{1}{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{1}}}\not{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{1}\perp}\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}_{1}}{2}+\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}_{2}}{2}\not{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{2}\perp}^{\dagger}\frac{1}{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}}\gamma^{\mu}\bigg](\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+(\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},-\omega_{2}})(Y_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}Y_{n_{1}})\bigg[\gamma^{\mu}\frac{1}{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{1}}}g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{n_{1}\perp}\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}_{1}}{2}+\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}_{2}}{2}g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{n_{2}\perp}\frac{1}{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}}\gamma^{\mu}\bigg](\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+(\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},-\omega_{2}})\bigg[\gamma^{\mu}\frac{1}{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{1}}}\Big(Y_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}\mathrm{i}\not{D}_{{\rm us}\perp}Y_{n_{1}}\Big)\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}_{1}}{2}+\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}_{2}}{2}\Big(Y_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i})\overleftarrow{\not{D}}_{{\rm us}\perp}Y_{n_{1}}\Big)\frac{1}{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}}\gamma^{\mu}\bigg](\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}})\bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\partial C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})}{\partial\omega_{1}}\,(\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},-\omega_{2}})\big(Y_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}\mathrm{i}\bar{n}_{1}\cdot D_{us}Y_{n_{1}}\big)\gamma^{\perp}_{\mu}(\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}})

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\partial C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})}{\partial\omega_{2}}\,(\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},-\omega_{2}})\big(Y_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i})\bar{n}_{2}\cdot\overleftarrow{D}_{us}Y_{n_{1}}\big)\gamma^{\perp}_{\mu}(\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}})\,.

To determine whether eq. (
4.38
) constrains the Wilson coefficients of subleading power operators we need to know whether there are other RPI invariant operators or mechanisms by which these operators can be generated. From ref.
[
111
]
we know there are other RPI operators whose leading expansion gives terms involving
\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\perp}
, so these structures are not constrained. This means we can simply ignore the
R_{n_{2}}^{(1)}
,
R_{n_{1}}^{(1)\dagger}
,
\not{\mathcal{B}}_{n_{1}\perp}
and
\not{\mathcal{B}}_{n_{2}\perp}
terms in eq. (
4.38
). We will study the
\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\perp}
operators independently using the helicity basis in
section
4.1.4
. A more tricky set of operators are the two that involve
\mathrm{i}\not{D}_{{\rm us}\perp}
. In
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
these operators are constrained by RPI with the Wilson coefficient given in eq. (
4.38
). However, in
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
there is another source that contributes to the matching onto these operators from the hard-collinear scale through
{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(1)}
, and hence their Wilson coefficients are not simply constrained by eq. (
4.38
) alone. We will leave these operators for now, and take them up again in detail in
section
4.1.5
below.

The remaining operators in eq. (
4.38
) are constrained by RPI when matched onto
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
. In this procedure ultrasoft fields become soft, so the Wilson lines
Y_{n_{i}}\to S_{n_{i}}
, and
{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot D_{\rm us}\to{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot D_{s}\sim\lambda
, so the last two terms in eq. (
4.38
) also contribute at NLP. For the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
operators with
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
, writing the contributions to
{\cal L}_{h}^{(1)}
with currents following eq. (
4.22
) we find

\displaystyle J^{(1)\mu}_{\mathcal{P}_{\perp}}(0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,\sum_{f}C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})\,\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}_{\perp}}^{(1)\mu}(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\,,

(4.39)

\displaystyle J^{(1)\mu}_{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}_{\perp}}(0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,\sum_{f}C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})\,\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}_{\perp}}^{(1)\mu}(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\,,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}_{\perp}}^{(1)\mu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\omega_{1}}\big[\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},-\omega_{2}}(S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}})\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\not{\mathcal{P}}_{n_{1}\perp}\,\not{{\bar{n}}}_{1}\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\big]\,,

(4.40)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{\dagger}}^{(1)\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\omega_{2}}\big[\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},-\omega_{2}}\,\not{{\bar{n}}}_{2}\not{\mathcal{P}}^{\dagger}_{n_{2}\perp}\,\gamma^{\mu}\,(S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}})\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\big]\,.

Once we specialize to the back-to-back frame
n_{2}=\bar{n}_{1}
, which is needed for our factorization analysis, we have
\mathcal{P}_{n_{1}\perp}=\mathcal{P}_{n_{2}\perp}=\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
and these two operators give the complete basis of operators involving
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
at this order for a vector current. This follows from ref.
[
71
]
(appendix C), which counts four independent helicity operators, and the constraint from parity for our case reduces this down to two operators. The choice of the two operators in eq. (
4.40
) thus suffices.
10
10

10

Note that we do not need to include operators with
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
acting on solely on the soft Wilson lines, as in
[[\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{\mu}S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}]]
, since such terms can be written in terms of
\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})\mu}
s, and hence it is enough to include a complete basis of operators involving
\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})\mu}
s, which we will do.

An example of this for a different operator is given in greater detail in
section
4.1.4
.
We also note that

\big(J^{(1)\mu}_{\mathcal{P}_{\perp}}\big)^{\dagger}=J^{(1)\mu}_{\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{\dagger}}
.
The extra
{\cal P}_{\perp}
s do not change the renormalization structure, so just like at leading power we will not need to consider evanescent operator extensions of these operators at higher orders
[
107
]
.
The contributions of these operators to the NLP factorization formula will be derived in
section
4.6
.

For the
\mathrm{i}{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot D_{s}
terms in eq. (
4.38
) it is useful to use

S_{n_{i}}^{\dagger}\mathrm{i}{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot D_{s}S_{n_{i}}={\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot\mathrm{i}\partial_{s}+g{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{s}^{(n_{i})}\,,

(4.41)

where
\mathcal{B}_{s}^{(n_{i})}
is given in eq. (
3.9
). This allows us to write

\displaystyle S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}\mathrm{i}\bar{n}_{1}\cdot D_{us}S_{n_{1}}

\displaystyle=S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}S_{n_{1}}^{\dagger}\mathrm{i}\bar{n}_{1}\cdot D_{us}S_{n_{1}}=S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big({\bar{n}}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{i}\partial_{s}+g{\bar{n}}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{s}^{(n_{1})}\big)\,,

(4.42)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}\bar{n}_{2}\cdot\overleftarrow{D}_{us}S_{n_{1}}

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}\bar{n}_{2}\cdot\overleftarrow{D}_{us}S_{n_{2}}S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}=\big(-{\bar{n}}_{2}\cdot\mathrm{i}\overleftarrow{\partial}_{s}+g{\bar{n}}_{2}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{s}^{(n_{2})}\big)S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\,.

Again following the notation in eq. (
4.22
), this gives the subleading power currents

\displaystyle J^{(1)\mu}_{{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}}(0)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,\sum_{f}\bigg[\frac{\partial C_{f}^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})}{\partial\omega_{1}}\,\mathcal{O}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}}^{(1)\mu}(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})+\frac{\partial C_{f}^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})}{\partial\omega_{2}}\,\mathcal{O}_{{\bar{n}}_{2}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}}^{(1)\mu}(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\bigg],

\displaystyle J^{(1)\mu}_{{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot\mathrm{i}\partial_{s}}(0)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,\sum_{f}\bigg[\frac{\partial C_{f}^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})}{\partial\omega_{1}}\,\mathcal{O}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{i}\partial_{s}}^{(1)\mu}(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})+\frac{\partial C_{f}^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})}{\partial\omega_{2}}\,\mathcal{O}_{{\bar{n}}_{2}\cdot\mathrm{i}\partial_{s}}^{(1)\mu}(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\bigg]\,,

(4.43)

with the operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}}^{(1)\mu}

\displaystyle=\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},\omega_{2}}\big(S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}{\bar{n}}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{s}^{(n_{1})}\big)\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{1},\omega_{1}},\quad\mathcal{O}_{{\bar{n}}_{2}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}}^{(1)\mu}=\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},\omega_{2}}\big({\bar{n}}_{2}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{s}^{(n_{2})}S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big)\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{1},\omega_{1}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}\cdot\mathrm{i}\partial_{s}}^{(1)\mu}

\displaystyle=\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},\omega_{2}}\big(S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\mathrm{i}{\bar{n}}_{1}\cdot\partial_{s}\big)\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{1},\omega_{1}}\,,\quad\mathcal{O}_{{\bar{n}}_{2}\cdot\mathrm{i}\partial_{s}}^{(1)\mu}=\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},\omega_{2}}\big(-\mathrm{i}{\bar{n}}_{2}\cdot\overleftarrow{\partial}_{s}S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big)\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{1},\omega_{1}}\,.

(4.44)

Here the
{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot\partial_{s}
operators pick out residual soft momentum of
{\cal O}(\lambda)
that are carried by the collinear fields, beneath their large
n_{i}
-collinear momenta
{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot p_{n_{i}}\sim\lambda^{0}
. They parameterize the NLP impact of subleading soft momenta that flow through the hard loops in QCD which were integrated out to obtain
C^{(0)}
.
Again for the case of the back-to-back frame
n_{2}={\bar{n}}_{1}
and
{\bar{n}}_{2}=n_{1}
, these operators give a complete set for the vector current. This again follows from the enumeration of all allowed helicity operators in ref.
[
71
]
and imposing parity. Since in this frame
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}=Q
and
\tilde{q}^{2}=\omega_{1}\omega_{2}
, the derivatives of the Wilson coefficients will also (eventually, after taking the matrix elements) become identical, and hence can be pulled out as a common factor, giving

\displaystyle J^{(1)\mu}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny$(-)$}}}{{n}}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}}(0)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n_{1}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,\sum_{f}\frac{\partial C_{f}^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})}{\partial\omega_{1}}\,\mathcal{O}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny$\!\!\!(-)$}}}{{n_{1}}}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}}^{(1)\mu}(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\,,

(4.45)

\displaystyle J^{(1)\mu}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny$(-)$}}}{{n}}\cdot\mathrm{i}\partial_{s}}(0)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{n_{1}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,\sum_{f}\frac{\partial C_{f}^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})}{\partial\omega_{1}}\,\mathcal{O}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny$\!\!\!(-)$}}}{{n_{1}}}\cdot\mathrm{i}\partial_{s}}^{(1)\mu}(\tilde{p}_{1},\tilde{p}_{2})\,,

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny$\!\!\!(-)$}}}{{n_{1}}}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}}^{(1)\mu}

\displaystyle=\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},\omega_{2}}\Big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}{\bar{n}}_{1}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{s}^{(n_{1})}+n_{1}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{s}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\Big)\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{1},\omega_{1}},

(4.46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny$\!\!\!(-)$}}}{{n_{1}}}\cdot\mathrm{i}\partial_{s}}^{(1)\mu}

\displaystyle=\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},\omega_{2}}\Big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\mathrm{i}{\bar{n}}_{1}\cdot\partial_{s}-\mathrm{i}n_{1}\cdot\overleftarrow{\partial}_{s}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\Big)\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{1},\omega_{1}}\,.

The contributions of the currents in eq. (
4.45
) to the NLP factorization formula will be considered in
section
4.5
.

4.1.4
NLP Operators with a Collinear
\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\perp}

In this section we construct a complete basis of
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
operators involving the field structures
\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}{\cal B}_{n_{i}\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{2}}
. Since the allowed form of these operators is not constrained by RPI, we will rely more heavily on the helicity operator basis, and then convert these to a useful form for our analysis.

We begin by briefly reviewing the spinor helicity formalism and corresponding conventions
employed in refs.
[
107
,
71
]
that are needed for our analysis here.
We use the standard spinor helicity notation

\displaystyle\ket{p}\equiv\ket{p+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\gamma_{5}}{2}\,u(p)\,,

\displaystyle|p]

\displaystyle\equiv\ket{p-}=\frac{1-\gamma_{5}}{2}\,u(p)\,,

(4.47)

\displaystyle\bra{p}\equiv\bra{p-}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sgn}(p^{0})\,\bar{u}(p)\,\frac{1+\gamma_{5}}{2}\,,

\displaystyle[p|

\displaystyle\equiv\bra{p+}=\mathrm{sgn}(p^{0})\,\bar{u}(p)\,\frac{1-\gamma_{5}}{2}\,,

with
p
lightlike. Spinor products of two lightlike vectors
p
and
q
are denoted as

\displaystyle\braket{pq}=\braket{p{-}|q{+}}\,,\qquad[pq]=\braket{p{+}|q{-}}\,.

(4.48)

The polarization vector of an outgoing gluon with momentum
p
can be written as

\varepsilon_{+}^{\mu}(p,r)=\frac{\langle p+\rvert\gamma^{\mu}\rvert r+\rangle}{\sqrt{2}\langle rp\rangle}\,,\qquad\varepsilon_{-}^{\mu}(p,r)=-\frac{\langle p-\rvert\gamma^{\mu}\rvert r-\rangle}{\sqrt{2}[rp]}\,,

(4.49)

where
r\neq p
is an arbitrary lightlike reference vector.

Next, we define collinear quark and gluon fields of definite helicity as

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{A}_{n_{i}\pm}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon_{\mp\mu}(n_{i},{\bar{n}}_{i})\,\mathcal{B}^{A\mu}_{n_{i}\perp,\omega_{i}}\,,\quad\chi_{n_{i}\pm}^{a}=\frac{1\,\pm\,\gamma_{5}}{2}\chi_{n_{i},-\omega_{i}}^{a}\,,\quad\bar{\chi}_{n_{i}\pm}^{\bar{a}}=\bar{\chi}_{n_{i},-\omega_{i}}^{\bar{a}}\frac{1\,\mp\,\gamma_{5}}{2}\,,

(4.50)

where
i=1,\,2
and
A,\,a,\,\bar{a}
are color indices.
Note that in SCET we use
n_{i}
for each collinear direction in spinors, rather than the momentum for each particle.
Since fermions always come in pairs, it is also useful to define currents of definite helicity,

\displaystyle J_{n_{2}n_{1}\pm}^{\bar{a}b}

\displaystyle=\mp\sqrt{\frac{2}{\omega_{2}\omega_{1}}}\,\frac{\varepsilon_{\mp}^{\mu}(n_{2},n_{1})}{\braket{n_{1}{\mp}|n_{2}{\pm}}}\,\bar{\chi}^{\bar{a}}_{n_{2}\pm}\gamma_{\mu}\chi^{b}_{n_{1}\pm}\,.

(4.51)

The leptonic currents are defined analogously as

J_{e\,n_{5}n_{4}\pm}=\mp\sqrt{\frac{2}{\omega_{5}\omega_{4}}}\,\frac{\varepsilon_{\mp}^{\mu}(n_{5},n_{4})}{\braket{n_{4}{\mp}|n_{5}{\pm}}}\,\bar{e}_{n_{5}\pm}\gamma_{\mu}e_{n_{4}\pm}\,.

(4.52)

Since the directions of the leptons are fixed by the process,
we often suppress the explicit dependence on the lightcone vectors
and abbreviate
J_{e\,\pm}\equiv J_{e\,n_{5}n_{4}\pm}
, with the understanding
that the reference vectors
n_{4,5}
in eq. (
4.52
) are identified
with those of the incoming and outgoing lepton, respectively.

In total, there are eight operators involving a single collinear gluon field
\mathcal{B}_{\pm}

[
71
]
which matter for our analysis at NLP,
11
11

11

There are additional NLP operators in
[
71
]
which involve two quark fields in the same collinear direction or three
\mathcal{B}
fields. For our NLP analysis these operators do not contribute since when combined with the leading power operator the factorized matrix elements do not conserve fermion number.

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n_{1}\,n_{2}n_{1}-(-;\pm)}^{(1)A\bar{a}b}=g\,\mathcal{B}_{n_{1}-}^{A}\,J_{n_{2}n_{1}\,-}^{\bar{a}b}\,J_{e\pm}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n_{1}\,n_{2}n_{1}+(+;\pm)}^{(1)A\bar{a}b}=g\,\mathcal{B}_{n_{1}+}^{A}\,J_{n_{2}n_{1}\,+}^{\bar{a}b}\,J_{e\pm}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n_{2}\,n_{2}n_{1}+(-;\pm)}^{(1)A\bar{a}b}=g\,\mathcal{B}_{n_{2}+}^{A}\,J_{n_{2}n_{1}\,-}^{\bar{a}b}\,J_{e\pm}\,,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n_{2}\,n_{2}n_{1}-(+;\pm)}^{(1)A\bar{a}b}=g\,\mathcal{B}_{n_{2}-}^{A}\,J_{n_{2}n_{1}\,+}^{\bar{a}b}\,J_{e\pm}\,.

(4.53)

The operators
\mathcal{O}_{n_{i}\,n_{2}n_{1}\lambda_{3}(\lambda_{21};\pm)}
are labelled
by the direction
n_{i}
with
i=1,2
of the gluon field,
the directions
n_{2}n_{1}
of the quark fields,
the helicity
\lambda_{3}
of the gluon and helicity
\lambda_{21}
of the quark current,
and the helicity
\pm
of the lepton current.
For brevity, we have suppressed the explicit arguments of the operators,
which encompass the directions
n_{1,2}
, the large
{\cal O}(\lambda^{0})
label momenta
\{\omega_{1,\cdots,5}\}

and the residual position
b^{\mu}
.

The unique color structure of all operators in eq. (
4.1.4
) is

\bar{T}^{A\,a\bar{b}}=\bigl(T^{A}_{a\bar{b}}\bigr)
.
In this one-dimensional color space, the Wilson coefficients

C^{(1)}_{\lambda_{3}(\lambda_{21}:\lambda_{45})}(n_{1},n_{2};\{\omega_{i}\})

are simple functions rather than vectors in color space,
such that we can drop the color indices on the
C^{(1)}
.
A priori, the eight operators in eq. (
4.1.4
) are independent of each other.
However, they are related to each other using discrete symmetries, yielding only one independent coefficient for the vector current relevant for photon exchange.
Parity and charge conjugation invariant of QCD imply the following relations respectively
[
71
]
, where here we are being careful about the overall phase induced from the definition of the spinors,

\displaystyle C^{(1)}_{\lambda_{3}(\lambda_{21}:\lambda_{45})}(\omega_{3};\omega_{1},\omega_{2};\omega_{4},\omega_{5})

\displaystyle=-C^{(1)}_{-\lambda_{3}(-\lambda_{21}:-\lambda_{45})}(\omega_{3};\omega_{1},\omega_{2};\omega_{4},\omega_{5})\Big|_{\Braket{\cdot\cdot}\leftrightarrow[\cdot\cdot]}\,,

(4.54a)

\displaystyle C^{(1)}_{\lambda_{3}(\lambda_{21}:\lambda_{45})}(\omega_{3};\omega_{1},\omega_{2};\omega_{4},\omega_{5})

\displaystyle=-C^{(1)}_{\lambda_{3}(-\lambda_{21}:-\lambda_{45})}(\omega_{3};\omega_{2},\omega_{1};\omega_{5},\omega_{4})\,.

(4.54b)

Since we only consider the leading order in the electromagnetic coupling, the leptons couple only through the vector currents which satisfy

\displaystyle\langle n_{5}\,\pm\rvert\gamma^{\mu}\rvert n_{4}\,\pm\rangle=\langle n_{4}\,\mp\rvert\gamma^{\mu}\rvert n_{5}\,\mp\rangle\,.

(4.55)

Thus, the Wilson coefficients obey

\displaystyle C^{(1)}_{\lambda_{3}(\lambda_{21}:\lambda_{45})}(\omega_{3};\omega_{1},\omega_{2};\omega_{4},\omega_{5})

\displaystyle=C^{(1)}_{\lambda_{3}(\lambda_{21}:-\lambda_{45})}(\omega_{3};\omega_{1},\omega_{2};\omega_{5},\omega_{4})\,.

(4.56)

Since the leptons couple only through the vector current in eq. (
4.55
),
it can be factored out from the Wilson coefficients, allowing us to write

C^{(1)}_{\lambda_{3}(\lambda_{21}:\lambda_{45})}(\omega_{3};\omega_{1},\omega_{2};\omega_{4},\omega_{5})=\langle n_{5}\,\lambda_{45}\rvert\gamma_{\mu}\rvert n_{4}\,\lambda_{45}\rangle\,\braket{n_{1}{\lambda_{3}}|n_{2}{-\lambda_{3}}}\,C^{(1)\mu}_{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{21}}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3})\,.

(4.57)

For reasons discussed later, we also factored out
\braket{n_{1}{\lambda_{3}}|n_{2}{-\lambda_{3}}}
.

C^{(1)\mu}_{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{21}}
is now a vector which does not have dependence on the leptonic parts.

To further constrain
C^{(1)}_{\lambda_{3}(\lambda_{21}:\lambda_{45})}
, we also make use of the little group property. Instead of discussing the little group for each individual particle as usually done in the helicity amplitude literature, in SCET we consider little group scaling for each collinear sector.
Under the little group scaling for the
n_{i}
sector,

\displaystyle|n_{i}\rangle\to t_{i}|n_{i}\rangle\,,\qquad|n_{i}]\to t_{1}^{-1}|n_{i}]\,,

(4.58)

the one particle state with momentum
p
in the collinear-
n_{i}
sector with helicity
h
scales like

\left|p,h\right>\to t_{i}^{2h}\left|p,h\right>\,.

(4.59)

The Feynman rules for
\chi_{n_{i}\pm}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\pm}
(with outgoing momenta and positive outgoing collinear momentum labels) were given in Ref.
[
107
]

\displaystyle\bigl\langle 0\bigr\rvert\chi^{b}_{n_{i}\pm}\bigr\rvert q_{\pm}^{\bar{a}}(-p)\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta^{b{\bar{a}}}\,\tilde{\delta}({\tilde{p}}_{i}-p)\,\ket{(-p_{i})\pm}_{n_{i}}\,,

(4.60)

\displaystyle\bigl\langle q_{\pm}^{a}(p)\bigr\rvert\bar{\chi}^{\bar{b}}_{n_{i}\pm}\bigr\rvert 0\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta^{a\bar{b}}\,\tilde{\delta}({\tilde{p}}_{i}-p)\,{}_{n_{i}\!}\bra{p_{i}\pm}\,,

\displaystyle\bigl\langle 0\bigr\rvert\bar{\chi}^{\bar{b}}_{n_{i}\pm}\bigr\rvert\bar{q}_{\mp}^{a}(-p)\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta^{a\bar{b}}\,\tilde{\delta}({\tilde{p}}_{i}-p)\,{}_{n_{i}\!}\bra{(-p_{i})\pm}\,,

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\bar{q}_{\mp}^{\bar{a}}(p)\bigr\rvert\chi^{b}_{n_{i}\pm}\bigr\rvert 0\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta^{b{\bar{a}}}\,\tilde{\delta}({\tilde{p}}_{i}-p)\,\ket{p_{i}\pm}_{n_{i}}\,,

\displaystyle\langle g_{\pm}^{A}(p)|\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\pm}^{B}|0\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta^{AB}\tilde{\delta}({\tilde{p}}_{i}-p)\,,

(4.61)

\displaystyle\langle 0|\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\pm}^{B}|g_{\mp}^{A}(-p)\rangle

\displaystyle=\delta^{AB}\tilde{\delta}({\tilde{p}}_{i}-p).

From these we can conclude that the quark fields
\chi_{n_{i}\pm}
does not scale since quark state scales in the same way as spinor, while the gluon field should scale in the same way as the gluon state,
\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\pm}\to t_{i}^{\pm 2}\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\pm}
.

The subleading power currents must be invariant under the little group scaling for each collinear sector,
so the scaling of the Wilson coefficients must be opposite to that of their corresponding operators.
Specifically, under independent actions of the little group,

\displaystyle|n_{1}\rangle\to t_{1}|n_{1}\rangle\,,\qquad|n_{1}]\to t_{1}^{-1}|n_{1}]\,,\qquad|n_{2}\rangle\to t_{2}|n_{2}\rangle\,,\qquad|n_{2}]\to t_{2}^{-1}|n_{2}]\,,

(4.62)

the polarization vectors, gluon and quark fields, and quark currents transform as

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\pm}^{\mu}(n_{i},r)

\displaystyle\to t_{i}^{\mp 2}\varepsilon_{\pm}^{\mu}(n_{i},r)\,,\qquad\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\pm}^{a}

\displaystyle\to t_{i}^{\pm 2}\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\pm}^{a}\,,

\displaystyle\chi_{n_{i}\pm}^{a}

\displaystyle\to\chi_{n_{i}\pm}^{a}\,,\hskip 48.36958ptJ_{n_{2}n_{1}\pm}^{\bar{a}b}

\displaystyle\to t_{2}^{\pm}t_{1}^{\mp}J_{n_{2}n_{1}\pm}^{\bar{a}b}\,.

(4.63)

The eight helicity operators eq. (
4.1.4
) then transform as

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n_{1}\,n_{2}n_{1}-(-;\pm)}^{(1)A\bar{a}b}\to\frac{1}{t_{2}t_{1}}\,\mathcal{O}_{n_{1}\,n_{2}n_{1}-(-;\pm)}^{(1)A\bar{a}b}\,,\quad

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n_{1}\,n_{2}n_{1}+(+;\pm)}^{(1)A\bar{a}b}\to t_{2}t_{1}\,\mathcal{O}_{n_{1}\,n_{2}n_{1}+(+;\pm)}^{(1)A\bar{a}b}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n_{2}\,n_{2}n_{1}+(-;\pm)}^{(1)A\bar{a}b}\to t_{2}t_{1}\,\mathcal{O}_{n_{2}\,n_{2}n_{1}+(-;\pm)}^{(1)A\bar{a}b}\,,\quad

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n_{2}\,n_{2}n_{1}-(+;\pm)}^{(1)A\bar{a}b}\to\frac{1}{t_{2}t_{1}}\,\mathcal{O}_{n_{2}\,n_{2}n_{1}-(+;\pm)}^{(1)A\bar{a}b}\,.

(4.64)

Notice that the scaling of these helicity operators is exactly canceled by the scaling of
\braket{n_{1}{\lambda_{3}}|n_{2}{-\lambda_{3}}}
in eq. (
4.57
). Therefore,
C^{(1)\mu}_{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{21}}
does not scale under the little group scaling.

The possible structure for the
\mu
index in
C^{(1)\mu}_{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{21}}
includes
\varepsilon_{\pm}^{\mu}(n_{1},n_{2})
,
\varepsilon_{\pm}^{\mu}(n_{2},n_{1})
,
n_{1}^{\mu}
and
n_{2}^{\mu}
, together with coefficients that are functions of spinor brackets
\left<n_{1}n_{2}\right>
and
\left[n_{1}n_{2}\right]
, and label momenta
\omega_{1}
,
\omega_{2}
,
\omega_{3}
.
However, the scaling of
\varepsilon_{\pm}^{\mu}
in eq. (
4.1.4
) can never be canceled by coefficients constructed using spinor brackets
\left<n_{1}n_{2}\right>
and
\left[n_{1}n_{2}\right]
which scale like
(t_{2}t_{1})^{\pm}
. Therefore,
C^{(1)\mu}_{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{21}}
can only contain terms proportional to
n_{1}^{\mu}
and
n_{2}^{\mu}
, which do not scale under the little group.

Further, we notice that
\braket{n_{1}n_{2}}[n_{1}n_{2}]=-2(n_{1}\cdot n_{2})
. That
C^{(1)\mu}_{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{21}}
does not scale then also imply that it is invariant when exchanging
\ket{\cdot\cdot}
and
\left|\cdot\cdot\right]
.
Applying eq. (
4.54a
) to eq. (
4.57
), we obtain

\displaystyle C^{(1)}_{\lambda_{3}(\lambda_{21}:\lambda_{45})}

\displaystyle=-\left[\langle n_{5}\,-\!\!\lambda_{45}\rvert\gamma_{\mu}\rvert n_{4}\,-\!\!\lambda_{45}\rangle\braket{n_{1}\,{-\lambda_{3}}|n_{2}\,{\lambda_{3}}}C^{(1)\mu}_{-\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{21}}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3})\right]_{\ket{\cdot\cdot}\leftrightarrow\left|\cdot\cdot\right]}

\displaystyle=-\langle n_{5}\,\lambda_{45}\rvert\gamma_{\mu}\rvert n_{4}\,\lambda_{45}\rangle\braket{n_{1}\,{\lambda_{3}}|n_{2}\,{-\lambda_{3}}}C^{(1)\mu}_{-\lambda_{3}-\lambda_{21}}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\braket{n_{1}{\lambda_{3}}|n_{2}{-\lambda_{3}}}}{\braket{n_{1}{-\lambda_{3}}|n_{2}{\lambda_{3}}}}C^{(1)}_{-\lambda_{3}(-\lambda_{21}:\lambda_{45})}\,,

(4.65)

where we suppressed the arguments of
C^{(1)}
.
Combining all Wilson coefficients with the corresponding operator from eq. (
4.1.4
),
we obtain the combinations

\displaystyle C^{(1)}_{-({-}:\pm)}g\mathcal{B}_{n_{1}-}J_{n_{2}n_{1}-}J_{e\pm}-C^{(1)}_{+(+:\pm)}g\mathcal{B}_{n_{1}+}J_{n_{2}n_{1}+}J_{e\pm}

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{(1)}_{-({-}:\pm)}}{\braket{n_{1}n_{2}}}\Bigl(\braket{n_{1}n_{2}}g\mathcal{B}_{n_{1}-}J_{n_{2}n_{1}-}-[n_{1}n_{2}]\,g\mathcal{B}_{n_{1}+}J_{n_{2}n_{1}+}\Bigr)J_{e\pm}\,,

(4.66)

\displaystyle C^{(1)}_{-(+:\pm)}g\mathcal{B}_{n_{2}-}J_{n_{2}n_{1}+}J_{e\pm}-C^{(1)}_{+({-}:\pm)}g\mathcal{B}_{n_{2}+}J_{n_{2}n_{1}-}J_{e\pm}

\displaystyle=\frac{C^{(1)}_{-(+:\pm)}}{\braket{n_{1}n_{2}}}\Bigl(\braket{n_{1}n_{2}}g\mathcal{B}_{n_{2}-}J_{n_{2}n_{1}+}-[n_{1}n_{2}]g\mathcal{B}_{n_{2}+}J_{n_{2}n_{1}-}\Bigr)J_{e\pm}

(4.67)

As a consequence, the
g\mathcal{B}_{n_{1}\lambda_{3}}J_{n_{2}n_{1}\lambda_{12}}
currents only appear in the linear combinations

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{B}1}

\displaystyle\equiv\sqrt{\frac{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}{2}}\bigl(\braket{n_{1}n_{2}}g\mathcal{B}_{n_{1}-}J_{n_{2}n_{1}-}-[n_{1}n_{2}]\,g\mathcal{B}_{n_{1}+}J_{n_{2}n_{1}+}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bar{\chi}_{\bar{n}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{n_{1}\perp,\omega_{3}}\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{B}2}

\displaystyle\equiv\sqrt{\frac{\omega_{1}\omega_{2}}{2}}\bigl(\braket{n_{1}n_{2}}g\mathcal{B}_{n_{2}-}J_{n_{2}n_{1}+}-[n_{1}n_{2}]\,g\mathcal{B}_{n_{2}+}J_{n_{2}n_{1}-}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bar{\chi}_{\bar{n}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{\bar{n}_{1},\omega_{3}}\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\,,

(4.68)

with Wilson coefficients specified by eq. (
4.1.4
).
As usual, the quark flavors are kept implicit both in the operator and the quark fields.
For the final equalities in eq. (
4.1.4
) we have taken
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
to be back-to-back, so that
n_{2}=\bar{n}_{1}
, which enables us to use the completeness relation for polarization vectors

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\lambda=\pm}\epsilon^{\lambda}_{\mu}(n_{i},\bar{n}_{i})\left(\epsilon^{\lambda}_{\nu}(n_{i},\bar{n}_{i})\right)^{*}=-g_{\mu\nu}+\frac{n_{i\mu}\bar{n}_{i\nu}+n_{i\nu}\bar{n}_{i\mu}}{n_{i}\cdot\bar{n}_{i}}=-g^{\perp}_{\mu\nu}(n_{i},{\bar{n}}_{i})\,.

(4.69)

To summarize the discussion so far, starting from the helicity operator basis with a priori eight independent operators as given in eq. (
4.1.4
),
using C/P invariance, the choice of vector current, and little group scaling we have shown that there are only two independent operators given in eq. (
4.1.4
).
Falling back to the standard SCET notation, we define the corresponding hard currents as

\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{B}1}^{(1)\mu}(0)

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\int\!\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathrm{d}\omega_{3}\,\,\sum_{f}C^{(1)\mu}_{1}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3})\,\mathcal{O}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{B}1}\left({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3}\right),

(4.70a)

\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{B}2}^{(1)\mu}(0)

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\int\!\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathrm{d}\omega_{3}\,\,\sum_{f}C^{(1)\mu}_{2}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3})\,\mathcal{O}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{B}2}\left({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3}\right),

(4.70b)

where the sum runs over all quark flavors
f
.
The label momenta are given by
{\tilde{p}}_{1}^{\mu}=\omega_{1}\frac{n_{1}^{\mu}}{2}
and
{\tilde{p}}_{2}^{\mu}=\omega_{2}\frac{n_{2}^{\mu}}{2}
,
with
{\tilde{p}}_{3}^{\mu}=\omega_{3}\frac{n_{1}^{\mu}}{2}
in eq. (
4.70a
) and
{\tilde{p}}_{3}^{\mu}=\omega_{3}\frac{n_{2}^{\mu}}{2}
in eq. (
4.70b
).

As argued above,
C^{(1)\mu}_{i}
can only depend on
n_{1}^{\mu}
and
n_{2}^{\mu}
.
Another constraint arises from current conservation
q_{\mu}J_{i}^{(1)\mu}=0
,
where at this order
q^{\mu}=\tilde{p}_{1}^{\mu}+\tilde{p}_{2}^{\mu}+\tilde{p}_{3}^{\mu}+{\cal O}(\lambda)
.
This implies that
C^{(1)\mu}_{1}
is proportional to
\tilde{p}_{1}^{\mu}-\tilde{p}_{2}^{\mu}+\tilde{p}_{3}^{\mu}
,
while
C^{(1)\mu}_{2}
is proportional to
\tilde{p}_{1}^{\mu}-\tilde{p}_{2}^{\mu}-\tilde{p}_{3}^{\mu}
.
Factoring these coefficients out, we have

\displaystyle C^{(1)\mu}_{1}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega_{2}n_{2}^{\mu}-(\omega_{1}+\omega_{3})n_{1}^{\mu}}{(\omega_{1}+\omega_{3})\omega_{2}}C^{(1)}_{1}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi_{1})=\frac{2\left[{\tilde{p}}_{2}^{\mu}-({\tilde{p}}_{1}^{\mu}+{\tilde{p}}_{3}^{\mu})\right]}{\tilde{q}^{2}}C^{(1)}_{1}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi_{1})\,,

(4.71)

\displaystyle C^{(1)\mu}_{2}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{(\omega_{2}+\omega_{3})n_{2}^{\mu}-\omega_{1}n_{1}^{\mu}}{\omega_{1}(\omega_{2}+\omega_{3})}C^{(1)}_{2}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi_{2})=\frac{2\left[({\tilde{p}}_{2}^{\mu}+{\tilde{p}}_{3}^{\mu})-{\tilde{p}}_{1}^{\mu}\right]}{\tilde{q}^{2}}C^{(1)}_{2}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi_{2})\,.

The normalization is chosen such that the scalar coefficients
C^{(1)}_{i}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi_{i})
are dimensionless.
Momentum conservation and RPI imply that they only depend on
\tilde{q}^{2}
and
\xi_{i}
, where

\displaystyle C^{(1)}_{1}:

\displaystyle\qquad\tilde{q}^{2}=(\omega_{1}+\omega_{3})\omega_{2}\frac{n_{1}\cdot n_{2}}{2}\,,\qquad\xi_{1}=\frac{\omega_{3}}{\omega_{1}+\omega_{3}}\,,

\displaystyle C^{(1)}_{2}:

\displaystyle\qquad\tilde{q}^{2}=(\omega_{2}+\omega_{3})\omega_{1}\frac{n_{1}\cdot n_{2}}{2}\,,\qquad\xi_{2}=\frac{\omega_{3}}{\omega_{2}+\omega_{3}}\,.

(4.72)

Now using charge invariance, eq. (
4.54b
), we obtain

\displaystyle C^{(1)\mu}_{2}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3})=-C^{(1)\mu}_{1}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3})\Big|_{\omega_{1}\leftrightarrow\omega_{2},\,n_{1}\leftrightarrow n_{2}}\,.

(4.73)

This implies that the two Wilson coefficients are in fact equal! We
will denote the single independent coefficient as

C^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\,\xi)\equiv C^{(1)}_{1}(\tilde{q}^{2},\,\xi)=C^{(1)}_{2}(\tilde{q}^{2},\,\xi)\,.

(4.74)

So far we have been suppressing the dependence of the coefficient on the quark flavor.
Restoring the flavor index
f
, we can extract the
quark charge similar to the leading-power Wilson coefficient in eq. (
4.1.2
),

\displaystyle C_{f}^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi)=Q_{f}\,C_{q}^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi)+\sum_{f^{\prime}}Q_{f^{\prime}}C_{vf^{\prime}}^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi)\,.

(4.75)

Since the flavor index is easy to restore, we will continue keep it implicit below.

The results for the form of these Wilson coefficients must also be consistent with those obtained purely in
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
.
The form of eq. (
4.71
) is consistent with the tree-level matching in eqs. (5.16) and (5.21) of ref.
[
71
]
. At tree-level we simply have
C_{q}^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi)=1
and
C_{vf^{\prime}}^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi)=0
.
Results for the anomalous dimension of these coefficients have also been obtained at NLL in Ref.
[
70
,
75
]
, which determines the form of scale dependent logarithmic terms in the matching coefficients at
{\cal O}(\alpha_{s})
.

We have not yet considered whether the Wilson coefficients
C_{q}^{(1)}
are real. For SIDIS it is known that the leading power coefficient
C^{(0)}
is real due to the space-like kinematics
\tilde{q}^{2}<0
. The coefficient is dimensionless and only involves logarithms of
\ln[(-\tilde{q}^{2}-i0)/\mu^{2}]
which are real. This is also clear from the relation of
C^{(0)}
to the space-like form factor in pure dimensional regularization. (In contrast, for Drell-Yan and
e^{+}e^{-}
annihilation the coefficient
C^{(0)}
is related to the time-like form factor and is complex.)
For the dimensionless coefficient
C_{q}^{(1)}
the anomalous dimension is real at LL
[
70
,
75
]
, but it is known that complex contributions begin to appear at NLO
[
96
]
. We will assume it is complex below.

So far, we have manipulated the operators and Wilson coefficients as in a
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
situation.
As described in
section
3
, to obtain the corresponding
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
operators,
we match this intermediate
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
theory onto
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
, which induces soft Wilson lines
analogous to the BPS field redefinition in eq. (
3.11
), but with soft Wilson lines. For subleading power
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
operators like these that contribute directly to
{\cal L}_{h}^{(1)}
without involving any time-ordered products, the (pre-BPS)
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}

\to

{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
matching amounts to making the replacements

\chi_{n,-\omega}\to S_{n}\chi_{n,-\omega}
and

\mathcal{B}_{n\perp,\omega_{3}}^{\mu}\to S_{n}\mathcal{B}_{n\perp,\omega_{3}}^{\mu}S_{n}^{\dagger}
.
Furthermore the structure of Wilson coefficients and operators for
{\cal L}_{h}^{(1)}
is exactly as in eq. (
4.70
), where we express the results with hadronic currents following the notation in eq. (
4.22
).
The corresponding Wilson coefficients are given by combining eq. (
4.71
),
with eq. (
4.74
).
In the back-to-back frame with
n_{2}=\bar{n}_{1}
we have

\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{B}1}^{(1)\mu}(0)

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{n_{1}}\int\!\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathrm{d}\omega_{3}\,\,\sum_{f}\frac{2\left({\tilde{p}}_{2}^{\mu}-{\tilde{p}}_{1}^{\mu}-{\tilde{p}}_{3}^{\mu}\right)}{\tilde{q}^{2}}C^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi_{1})\,\mathcal{O}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{B}1}\left({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3}\right),

(4.76)

\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{B}2}^{(1)\mu}(0)

\displaystyle=\!\sum_{n_{1}}\int\!\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathrm{d}\omega_{3}\,\,\sum_{f}\frac{2\left({\tilde{p}}_{2}^{\mu}+{\tilde{p}}_{3}^{\mu}-{\tilde{p}}_{1}^{\mu}\right)}{\tilde{q}^{2}}C^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi_{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{B}2}\left({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3}\right)\,.

with
\tilde{q}^{2},\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
defined in eq. (
4.1.4
), and
where eq. (
4.1.4
) yields the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{B}1}({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3})

\displaystyle=\big[\bar{\chi}_{\bar{n}_{1},-\omega_{2}}S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}_{1}}S_{n_{1}}\,g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{n_{1}\perp,\omega_{3}}S_{n_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\big]

\displaystyle=\big[\bar{\chi}_{\bar{n}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,(S_{\bar{n}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}})\,g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{n_{1}\perp,\omega_{3}}\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\big]\,,

(4.77a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{B}2}^{(1)}\left({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2},{\tilde{p}}_{3}\right)

\displaystyle=\big[\bar{\chi}_{\bar{n}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,S_{\bar{n}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{\bar{n}_{1}}\,g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{\perp\bar{n}_{1},\omega_{3}}\,S_{\bar{n}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\big]

\displaystyle=\big[\bar{\chi}_{\bar{n}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{\perp\bar{n}_{1},\omega_{3}}\,(S_{\bar{n}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}})\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\big]\,.

(4.77b)

Notice that the soft Wilson lines between the quark and gluon fields in the same collinear direction
cancel out as
S_{n}^{\dagger}S_{n}=1
, so that overall the soft gluons couple to only the global color charge of the combined set of collinear fields in that direction. This
leaves only the combination
(S_{\bar{n}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}})
that already appear at LP, which is a key step in the proof that these NLP operators will lead to a factorization formula having the same soft factor as at LP, rather than a genuinely new soft factor.

It is natural to ask whether loop corrections to the operators in eq. (
4.77
) will require the introduction of evanescent operators that vanish in 4-dimensions. The results of Ref.
[
70
]
imply that such operators are not needed for the one-loop anomalous dimension of our
{\cal O}(\lambda)
operators
{\cal O}^{(1)}_{{\cal B}i}
, which are multiplicatively renormalized. We anticipate that this will continue to the be the case at higher orders because when additional Dirac matrices from collinear loops are inserted in the product of operators
\not{\mathcal{B}}_{n\perp}\chi_{n}
, they can be reduced back to this form with
d
-dimensional Dirac algebra.

Note that unlike
C^{(0)}
, the new hard coefficient
C^{(1)}
in eq. (
4.76
) depends on extra momentum fractions
\xi_{i}
that determine the split of the large momentum between quark and gluon fields in the same collinear direction. Examples of these convolutions are known from subleading power factorization for light-cone distribution functions in
B
-physics, see for eg.
[
102
,
116
,
59
,
60
,
117
]
. Once the SIDIS matrix elements have been factorized at NLP, we can anticipate from the field structure that the corresponding subleading power TMD will also depends on
\xi
. Thus we can already infer the presence of an additional convolution integral over
\xi
, between the hard coefficient and subleading power TMD.

4.1.5
NLP Operators with a
\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}
, Hard and Hard-Collinear Contributions

Next we turn to deriving the hard scattering Lagrangians for the NLP
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
operators with the field structures
\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}{\cal B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})\mu}\chi_{n_{2}}
and
\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}{\cal B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{2})\mu}\chi_{n_{2}}
. It turns out that there are two sources for these operators, from integrating out fluctuations at the hard scale,
{\cal L}_{h}^{(1)}
, and at the hard-collinear scale,
{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(1)}
.

Hard scale contribution

For the hard scale contribution the structure of the operators is constrained by RPI, and follows from the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
operators given in the fifth line of eq. (
4.38
).
To discuss these terms it will be useful to first observe a few relations between operators in
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
.
In the back-to-back frame where
n_{2}={\bar{n}}_{1}
and
{\bar{n}}_{2}=n_{1}
the following relation between forms of the soft operator will be useful

\displaystyle\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}\mathrm{i}\not{D}_{s\perp}S_{n_{1}}\big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

(4.78)

\displaystyle\ \ =\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\,\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}\big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}+\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\,\big)\,\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\ \ =\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}\mathrm{i}\not{D}_{s\perp}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}=\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}+\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}\big)S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\ \ =\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}+\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big[\!\big[\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big]\!\big]\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big)\,\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\ \ =\frac{1}{2}\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}+S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}\big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\frac{1}{2}\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big[\!\big[\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big]\!\big]\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}+\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big)\,\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\,.

To obtain the last equality we have symmetrized over the results in the first and third equalities.
Similarly we have

\displaystyle\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}(-\mathrm{i})\overleftarrow{\not{D}}_{s\perp}S_{n_{1}}\big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

(4.79)

\displaystyle\ \ =\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}+\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}^{\dagger}\,\big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\ \ =\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}\big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}-\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big[\!\big[\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big]\!\big]\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}^{\dagger}\,\big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\ \ =\frac{1}{2}\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big(g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}+S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}\big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\big[\!\big[\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big]\!\big]\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}+\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}^{\dagger}\,\big(S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\,.

In eqs. (
4.78
) and (
4.79
) the double brackets in
\big[\!\big[\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big]\!\big]
indicate that the
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{\alpha}
derivative only acts inside, and for the latter equation we have used
\big[\!\big[S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}^{\dagger}\big]\!\big]=-\big[\!\big[\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big]\!\big]
.

For the contribution from the hard region, the Wilson coefficient of the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
operator is fixed by the reparameterization invariance arguments in
section
4.1.3
(which fixed the coefficients of the
\mathrm{i}\not{D}_{{\rm us}\perp}
terms) to be the LP coefficient
C^{(0)}
.
These constraints are inherited from the constraint in
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
because the physics from integrating out the hard scale fluctuations is the same in the two theories.
To obtain this relation we consider the fifth line of eq. (
4.38
) and make use of eq. (
4.78
) and eq. (
4.79
).
When matching onto
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
the terms with
\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}
in eq. (
4.78
) and
\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}^{\dagger}
in eq. (
4.79
) must be dropped since they are already included explicitly on the third line of eq. (
4.38
).
When we add eq. (
4.78
) and eq. (
4.79
) the terms involving
\big[\!\big[\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big]\!\big]
cancel out.
12
12

12

Note that we do not have to consider these terms as a potential additional independent operator that is not constrained by RPI, due to the integration by parts argument explained in
section
4.1.3
.
Thus the sum of operators on the fifth line of eq. (
4.38
) purely involves terms with a
\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}
or
\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{2})}
.
The Dirac structure appearing for these terms in eq. (
4.38
) can also be simplified further in the back-to-back frame, where after the above considerations we now have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{\,2})\>\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\bigg[\gamma^{\mu}\frac{1}{\overline{\mathcal{P}}}\gamma_{\perp}^{\alpha}\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}_{1}}{2}+\frac{\not{n}_{1}}{2}\gamma_{\perp}^{\alpha}\frac{1}{\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{\dagger}}\gamma^{\mu}\bigg]\Big(g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp\alpha}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}+S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp\alpha}^{(n_{1})}\Big)\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\ \ =\Big(\frac{{\bar{n}}_{1}^{\mu}}{2\omega_{1}}-\frac{n_{1}^{\mu}}{2\omega_{2}}\Big)C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{\,2})\>\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\Big(g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}+S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}\Big)\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\,.

(4.80)

To obtain the last line we used the projection relations on the SCET fermion fields given below eq. (
3.3
). Taken all together, we obtain the following hadronic current

\displaystyle J_{h\,{\mathcal{B}}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)\mu}(0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,\,\sum_{f}\frac{2(\tilde{p}_{2}^{\mu}-\tilde{p}_{1}^{\mu})}{\tilde{q}^{2}}\,C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})\,\mathcal{O}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}\left({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2}\right)\,,

(4.81)

where the subscript
h
indicates it arises as an
{\cal L}_{h}^{(1)}
contribution. Here
\tilde{q}^{2}=\omega_{1}\omega_{2}
and the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{(1)}_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}\left({\tilde{p}}_{1},{\tilde{p}}_{2}\right)\equiv\frac{1}{2}\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\,\Big(g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}+S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}\Big)\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\,.

(4.82)

Not that this operator is hermitian on its own if we take the dagger and use the freedom to exchange
n_{1}\leftrightarrow{\bar{n}}_{1}
and
\omega_{1}\leftrightarrow\omega_{2}
due to the sum and integrals. Indeed this is the same combination that we saw for the final
{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{n_{i}\perp}
operators in eq. (
4.76
), except that now the two terms have a common Wilson coefficient
C^{(0)}
that does not depend on the soft gluon’s momentum. By the same counting of helicity operators and use of symmetries carried out in
section
4.1.4
, we obtain that eq. (
4.82
) is the unique operator of this type at this order.

Hard-collinear scale contribution

Next we consider contributions to the
\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}{\cal B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})\mu}\chi_{n_{2}}
and
\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}{\cal B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{2})\mu}\chi_{n_{2}}
operators from integrating out fluctuations at the hard-collinear scale
p^{2}\sim Q^{2}\lambda
, denoted as
{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(1)}
contributions.
Here the analysis for the number and Dirac structure for the operators exactly parallels that with
{\cal B}_{n_{i}\perp}^{\mu}
from
section
4.1.4
. All the symmetry arguments that have been applied to determine the structure of
\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\perp}
-type operators in
section
4.1.4
can also be applied to
{\cal B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})}
. The key difference for the operators is that the soft Wilson lines appear in the definition of
{\cal B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})}
itself, as well as multiplying it, whereas for the collinear
{\cal B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})}
the definition contains collinear Wilson lines, and it is multiplied by soft Wilson lines. This does not change the constraints on the Dirac structures, so the operators obtained from
{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(1)}
must yield contributions of the same form as eq. (
4.82
).

To derive results in the form of a factorization theorem valid to all orders in
\alpha_{s}
for this
{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(1)}
term, we again utilize the framework of matching from
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
onto
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
. Using
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
all the hard-collinear contributions can be obtained by factorizing the time-ordered products in eq. (
3.18
) when they are considered with external
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
kinematics. In order to contribute to an offshell hard-collinear contribution, these time-ordered products must (after the BPS field redefinition) involve
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
Lagrangians
{\cal L}_{\rm I}^{(1,2)}
with an explicit ultrasoft quark or gluon field that can inject an ultrasoft momentum. These terms can be obtained from Ref.
[
58
]
. At the order we are working there are no contributions with ultrasoft quark fields since
\psi_{us}\sim\lambda^{3}
, and the power counting would limit us to having at most one such ultrasoft quark field, which then leads to vanishing contributions in the soft matrix element by fermion number conservation. Thus only Lagrangians with ultrasoft gluon fields
{\cal B}_{us\perp}^{(n_{i})\mu}\sim\lambda^{2}
will contribute. The decomposition of the subleading power
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
Lagrangians using these ultrasoft gluon fields can be found in Ref.
[
80
]
.

Although it appears that this analysis could be fairly complicated, it turns out that for the NLP terms in SIDIS there is a significant simplification. As explained in detail in
section
4.5
below, any term involving a

{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(0)}
does not contribute to the
W_{i}
structure functions that are of interest here, namely those which obtain their first non-zero contributions at NLP. Thus the only non-trivial time-ordered product that we will need from the list in eq. (
3.18
) is, in the back-to-back frame where
n_{2}=\bar{n}_{1}
,

\displaystyle T[{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(1)}{\cal L}_{\rm I}^{(1)}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1}}\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{4}x\>T\,{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(1)}(0)\Big({\cal L}_{n_{1}\,\rm I}^{(1)}(x)+{\cal L}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}\,\rm I}^{(1)}(x)\Big)\,.

(4.83)

Here the
{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(1)}
operators are just the
\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}{\cal P}_{\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{1}}
and
\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}{\cal B}_{n_{i}\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{1}}
terms given in eqs. (
4.39
) and (
4.70
) with
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
collinear fields. The relevant terms in the subleading power Lagrangian are

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{n\,{\rm I}}^{(1)}

\displaystyle={\cal L}_{\chi_{n}\mathcal{B}_{us}^{\perp}}^{(1)}+{\cal L}_{A_{n}\mathcal{B}_{us}^{\perp}}^{(1)}=g{\mathcal{B}}_{us\perp\alpha}^{(n)a}\Big(K_{\chi_{n}}^{\alpha a(1)}+K_{A_{n}}^{\alpha a(1)}\Big)\,,

(4.84)

where we can read off the required formula from Ref.
[
80
]
,

\displaystyle K_{\chi_{n}}^{\alpha a(1)}

\displaystyle=\bar{\chi}_{n}T^{a}\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{\alpha}\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}}{\bar{\mathcal{P}}}\chi_{n}+\bar{\chi}_{n}\Bigl(T^{a}\gamma^{\alpha}_{\perp}\frac{1}{\bar{\mathcal{P}}}g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{n\perp}+g\not{\mathcal{B}}_{n\perp}\frac{1}{\bar{\mathcal{P}}}\gamma_{\perp}^{\alpha}T^{a}\Bigr)\frac{\not{\bar{n}}}{2}\chi_{n}\,,

\displaystyle K_{A_{n}}^{\alpha a(1)}

\displaystyle=-if^{abc}\left\{\mathcal{B}^{\mu b}_{n\perp}[\mathcal{P}^{\mu}_{\perp}\mathcal{B}^{\alpha c}_{n\perp}]-\mathcal{B}^{\mu b}_{n\perp}[\mathcal{P}^{\alpha}_{\perp}\mathcal{B}^{\mu c}_{n\perp}]+[\bar{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{B}^{\alpha c}_{n\perp}]n\cdot\mathcal{B}^{b}_{n}\right\}+gf^{cbe}f^{ade}\mathcal{B}^{\mu c}_{n\perp}\mathcal{B}^{\alpha b}_{n\perp}\mathcal{B}^{\mu d}_{n\perp}

\displaystyle~~~\,+2\text{Tr}\left(\bar{c}_{n}[T^{a},[iD^{\perp}_{n\alpha},c_{n}]]\right)+2\text{Tr}\left(\bar{c}_{n}[\mathcal{P}_{\perp\alpha},[W_{n}T^{a}W^{\dagger}_{n},c_{n}]]\right)

\displaystyle~~~\,+2\zeta\text{Tr}\left([T^{a},A_{n\perp\alpha}][i\partial_{n}^{\nu},A_{n\nu}]\right)\,.

(4.85)

Here the contributions to the pure gluon action are written in a general covariant gauge with gauge parameter
\zeta
, and
c_{n}
are
n
-collinear ghost fields. Note that only transverse ultrasoft gluon fields,
{\mathcal{B}}_{us\perp}^{(n)}
, appear in the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
Lagrangian at this order, explaining why we only have the possibility of matching onto the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
operators of the form
\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})}\chi_{n_{1}}
.

Consider first the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}

\to

{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
matching for the
\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}{\cal P}_{\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{1}}
terms in
{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(1)}
. We find that all such contributions enter beyond
{\cal O}(\eta)
, ie. with
E\geq 1/2
in eq. (
3.13
). This occurs because even in the presence of
n
-collinear propagators and loops in
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
, the
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{\mu}
always gives an external momentum, and hence an extra suppression factor when matching to
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
. For example, consider the following tree level and one-loop contractions

\displaystyle\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{4}x\

\displaystyle[\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}{\not{\mathcal{P}}}_{\perp}\!\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\underbrace{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\,\,\overbracket{\!\!\!\chi_{n_{1}}](0)\Big[\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}\!\!\!\!}\,\,\,\,}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}g{\not{B}}_{us\perp}\frac{1}{\overline{\mathcal{P}}}(\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}+g{\not{B}}_{n_{1}\perp})\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}}{2}\chi_{n}\Big](x)\,,

(4.86)

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ \lambda\hskip 11.38092pt\lambda\hskip 28.45274pt\lambda^{-1}\hskip 22.76228pt\lambda\hskip 48.36958pt\lambda\hskip 51.21504pt\lambda\hskip 28.45274pt={\cal O}(\lambda^{4})}

\displaystyle\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\mathrm{d}^{4}y\

\displaystyle[\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}{\not{\mathcal{P}}}_{\perp}\!\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\underbrace{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\,\,\overbracket{\!\!\!\chi_{n_{1}}](0){\cal L}_{n}^{(0)}(y)\hskip-28.45274pt}\hskip 28.45274pt\hskip-19.91684pt\overbracket{\hskip 19.91684pt\hskip-17.07182pt\overbracket{\hskip 17.07182pt\Big[\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}\hskip-11.38092pt}\hskip 11.38092pt(\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}+g{\not{B}}_{n_{1}\perp})\hskip-19.91684pt}\hskip 19.91684pt}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1}{\overline{\mathcal{P}}}g{\not{B}}_{us\perp}\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}}{2}\chi_{n}\Big](x)\,,

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ \lambda\hskip 11.38092pt\lambda\hskip 76.82234pt\lambda^{-1/2}\hskip 76.82234pt\lambda\hskip 31.2982pt\lambda\hskip 14.22636pt={\cal O}(\lambda^{7/2})}

In orange we show the power counting obtained when matching to
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
, which is
{\cal O}(\lambda^{2})
and
{\cal O}(\lambda^{3/2})
suppressed relative to the leading power hard scattering operator. The power counting for the contractions is given together and includes factors of
\lambda
from the hard-collinear space-time integrations. A similar suppression is obtained for any other contractions involving the
\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}{\cal P}_{\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{1}}
current, so these terms only start to contribute beyond the
{\cal O}(\lambda)
NLP corrections that we are considering.

Next we consider the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}

\to

{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
matching from the
\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{n_{i}\perp}\chi_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}
terms in
{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(1)}
.
Just like the previous case, at tree level the time-ordered products here again only yield contributions beyond leading power, with
E\geq 1
. This occurs because only one pair of fields are contracted from each of
{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(1)}
and
{\cal L}_{\rm I}^{(1)}
in eq. (
4.83
), so we end up with the same type of suppression observed in the first example of eq. (
4.86
). On the other hand, once loops are considered a contribution at NLP can now be obtained. This occurs because we can carry out contractions of all fields in loops at the hard-collinear scale except for the fields
\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}\mathcal{B}_{us\perp}^{\mu}\chi_{n_{1}}
, and hence end up with a
{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(1)}
contribution that matches onto the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
operators
\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})}\chi_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}
