. As an example of a one-loop contraction that contributes at NLP we have

\displaystyle\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{4}x\

\displaystyle[\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\underbrace{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hskip 8.5359pt\overbracket{\hskip-8.5359pt{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{n_{1}\perp}\,\,\,\overbracket{\!\!\!\chi_{n_{1}}](0)\Big[\bar{\chi}_{n_{1}}\hskip-11.38092pt}\hskip 11.38092pt(\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}+g{\not{B}}_{n_{1}\perp})\hskip-19.91684pt}\hskip 19.91684pt}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1}{\overline{\mathcal{P}}}g{\not{B}}_{us\perp}\frac{\not{{\bar{n}}}}{2}\chi_{n}\Big](x)\,,

(4.87)

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}{\ \lambda\hskip 81.09052pt\lambda^{0}\hskip 83.93553pt\lambda\hskip 22.76228pt\lambda\hskip 14.22636pt={\cal O}(\lambda^{3})}

where again we have shown the power counting in
\lambda
after the result is matched to
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
.
This example has contractions that yield an
n_{1}
-hard-collinear loop. There are mirror contributions from
{\bar{n}}_{1}
-hard-collinear loops as well involving the operator
\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}\perp}\chi_{n_{1}}
.
If we consider the complete basis of
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
operators that can be obtained from this hard-collinear matching, then we can use the fact that the result must involve a
{\mathcal{B}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})\mu}
, and is constrained by the same symmetries that we already considered when carrying out the matching for
{\cal L}_{h}^{(1)}
. This implies that these
{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(1)}
contributions will match onto the same two operators that appears above in eq. (
4.82
).
We have confirmed that the sum of hard-collinear one-loop diagrams in
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
is non-zero from contractions like the one in eq. (
4.87
), and that it yields a non-zero contribution to the NLP matching onto
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
that gives either one of the two operators in eq. (
4.82
).

To formulate the result from the hard-collinear loop corrections in a form that is valid to all orders in
\alpha_{s}
, we first note that the
{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(1)}
operators
\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},\omega_{1}}{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{n_{i}\perp}\chi_{n_{1},\omega_{2}}
have hard coefficients
C^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi)
whose form was determined in
section
4.1.4
, see eq. (
4.70
) with eqs. (
4.71
) and (
4.74
).
For an
n_{1}
-hard-collinear loop the matching result depends on
\xi_{1}
, and on the
p_{n_{1}}^{-}=\omega_{1}\sim\lambda^{0}
and
p_{s}^{+}\sim\lambda
momentum of the
n
-collinear and soft fields in eq. (
4.82
). For an
{\bar{n}}_{1}
-hard-collinear loop the result depends on
\xi_{2}
, and the
p_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{+}=\omega_{2}\sim\lambda^{0}
of the
{\bar{n}}_{1}
-collinear field and
p_{s}^{-}\sim\lambda
momentum of the soft field.
These hard-collinear loops give rise to a non-trivial scalar matching coefficient function
J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}}
. Thus the all orders form for the matching is

\displaystyle J^{(1)\mu}_{{\rm hc}\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}(0)=\sum_{n_{1}}\int\!\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\>\sum_{f}\frac{(\tilde{p}_{2}^{\mu}-\tilde{p}_{1}^{\mu})}{\tilde{q}^{2}}\,

(4.88)

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{\int\!\!\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\ C^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi_{1})\int\!\!\mathrm{d}p_{s}^{+}\>J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}(\omega_{1}p_{s}^{+},\xi_{1})\,\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\big[S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}g{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}\big]_{p_{s}^{+}}\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\int\!\!\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\ C^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi_{2})\int\!\!\mathrm{d}p_{s}^{-}\,J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}(\omega_{2}p_{s}^{-},\xi_{2})\,\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\big[g{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big]_{p_{s}^{-}}\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1}}\int\!\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\>\sum_{f}\frac{(\tilde{p}_{2}^{\mu}-\tilde{p}_{1}^{\mu})}{\tilde{q}^{2}}\,

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{\int\!\!\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\ C^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi_{1})\int\!\!\frac{\mathrm{d}b_{s}^{-}}{\omega_{1}}\,\tilde{J}_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}(b_{s}^{-}/\omega_{1},\xi_{1})\,\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\big[S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}g{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}\big](b_{s}^{-})\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\int\!\!\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\ C^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi_{2})\int\!\!\frac{\mathrm{d}b_{s}^{+}}{\omega_{2}}\,\tilde{J}_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}(b_{s}^{+}/\omega_{2},\xi_{2})\,\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\big[g{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big](b_{s}^{+})\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\bigg\}.

Here the subscripts
p_{s}^{\pm}
fix the fields in the
[\cdots]_{p_{s}^{\pm}}
to have these momenta, for example

stuff

\big[S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}g{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}\big]_{p_{s}^{+}}=\big[\delta(p_{s}^{+}-n_{1}\cdot\mathrm{i}\partial_{s})S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}g{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}\big]
.
Only a single dimensionless function
J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}}(s,\xi)
appears in eq. (
4.88
) due to the symmetry between
n_{1}
and
{\bar{n}}_{1}
hard-collinear loops. When renormalized in the
{\overline{\rm MS}}
scheme
J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}}(s,\xi,\mu)
will involve logarithms like
\ln(s/\mu^{2})
, and we note that the scale of its first argument,
s\sim Q^{2}\lambda
, is hard-collinear.
The dimension-
(-2)
matching coefficients
\tilde{J}_{\mathcal{B}_{s}}
are the Fourier transform of the momentum space coefficients
J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}}
with respect to the soft momenta,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\!\!\mathrm{d}p_{s}^{+}\>e^{-ib_{s}^{-}p_{s}^{+}/2}\>J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}(p_{s}^{+}\omega_{1},\xi_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega_{1}}\tilde{J}_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}(b_{s}^{-}/\omega_{1},\xi_{1})\,.

(4.89)

Total Contribution

To conclude this section we add the hard and hard-collinear contributions from eqs. (
4.81
) and (
4.88
) to obtain the final
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
current with
{\cal B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})}
insertions in the following form:

\displaystyle J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)\mu}(0)

\displaystyle=J_{h\,\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)\mu}(0)+J_{{\rm hc}\,\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)\mu}(0)

(4.90)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,\sum_{f}\frac{(\tilde{p}_{2}^{\mu}-\tilde{p}_{1}^{\mu})}{\tilde{q}^{2}}\,

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigg\{\int\!\!\frac{\mathrm{d}b_{s}^{-}}{\omega_{1}}\,C_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)}\big(\tilde{q}^{2},b_{s}^{-}/\omega_{1}\big)\,\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\big[S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}g{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}\big](b_{s}^{-})\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}

\displaystyle\quad\ \ \ +\int\!\!\frac{\mathrm{d}b_{s}^{+}}{\omega_{2}}\,C_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)}\big(\tilde{q}^{2},b_{s}^{+}/\omega_{2}\big)\,\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\big[g{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big](b_{s}^{+})\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,\sum_{f}\frac{(\tilde{p}_{2}^{\mu}-\tilde{p}_{1}^{\mu})}{\tilde{q}^{2}}\,\int\!\!\mathrm{d}\hat{b}_{s}\,C_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)}\big(\tilde{q}^{2},\hat{b}_{s}\big)\,

\displaystyle\quad\times\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}_{1},-\omega_{2}}\Big\{\big[S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}g{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{s\perp}^{(n_{1})}\big](\omega_{1}\hat{b}_{s})+\big[g{\not{\mathcal{B}}}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}}_{1})}S_{{\bar{n}}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\big](\omega_{2}\hat{b}_{s})\Big\}\,\chi_{n_{1},-\omega_{1}}\,,

where it was convenient to introduce the common integration variable
\hat{b}_{s}
, which has dimension-
-2
. The combined matching coefficients containing both hard and hard-collinear contributions are

\displaystyle C_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)}\big(\tilde{q}^{2},\hat{b}_{s}\big)

\displaystyle=C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})\,\delta(\hat{b}_{s})\ +\ \int\!\!\mathrm{d}\xi\>C^{(1)}(\tilde{q}^{2},\xi)\,\tilde{J}_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}(\hat{b}_{s},\xi)\,.

(4.91)

4.2
Factorization with Leading Power Currents

In this section, we calculate the contributions from the LP current
J^{(0)}
to the factorization theorem.
This will both reproduce the well-known LP factorization theorem and induce kinematic corrections at NLP.
We start from the definition of the hadronic tensor in eq. (
4
), evaluated with the LP current
J^{(0)}
,

\displaystyle W_{J^{(0)}}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\SumInt_{X}\,\delta^{4}(q+P_{N}-P_{h}-P_{X})\braket{N|J^{(0)\,\dagger\,\mu}(0)|h,X}\braket{h,X|J^{(0)\,\nu}(0)|N}\,.

Note that here, we have not yet absorbed the
\delta
function by shifting the current
as in eq. (
4
), as it will be illustrative to manipulate the
\delta
function directly,
rather than having to insert the hard current to gain access to its
b
dependence.
Of course, in the end both approaches give the same result.

The state
X
and its phase space integral
\SumInt_{X}
are factorized into the two collinear and one soft sectors,

\ket{X}=\ket{X_{n}}\ket{X_{\bar{n}}}\ket{X_{s}}\,,\qquad\qquad\SumInt_{X}=\SumInt_{X_{n}}\SumInt_{X_{\bar{n}}}\SumInt_{X_{s}}\,,

(4.100)

so that the collinear and soft fields in the SCET operators overlap only with
the corresponding counterpart of the state
X
.
We also decompose
P_{X}
as

P_{X}=P_{X_{n}}+P_{X_{\bar{n}}}+P_{X_{s}}\,,

(4.101)

and define the momenta of the initial and final state partons as

p_{a}=P_{N}-P_{X_{n}}\,,\qquad p_{b}=P_{h}+P_{X_{\bar{n}}}\,.

(4.102)

This definition is motivated by the observation that the
n
-collinear radiation
X_{n}

dominantly arises from radiation off the incoming hadron
N
, and thus by momentum conservation
the struck parton carries all momentum of
P_{N}
that is not radiated away by
X_{n}
,
and similarly for the final-state momentum
p_{b}
.
The
\delta
function in eq. (
4.2
) is not homogeneous in the power counting,
as is easy to see from the scalings
q\sim Q(1,1,\lambda)
,
p_{a}\sim Q(\lambda^{2},1,\lambda)
and
p_{b}\sim Q(1,\lambda^{2},\lambda)
.
Expanding it to NLP, we obtain
13
13

13

There are two alternative ways to treat the expansion in eq. (
4.103
) which we discuss in
section
4.5.3
.

\displaystyle\delta^{4}(q+P_{N}-P_{h}-P_{X})

\displaystyle=\delta^{4}(q+p_{a}-p_{b}-P_{X_{s}})

(4.103)

\displaystyle=\bigl[1+{\bar{n}}\cdot P_{X_{s}}\partial_{\omega_{a}}-n\cdot P_{X_{s}}\partial_{\omega_{b}}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\bigr]

\displaystyle\quad\times 2\delta(\omega_{a}-{\bar{n}}\cdot p_{a})\,\delta(\omega_{b}-n\cdot p_{b})\,\delta^{(2)}(\vec{q}_{T}+\vec{p}_{a,T}-\vec{p}_{b,T}-\vec{P}_{X_{s},T})\,.

Here, we parameterized
q^{\mu}
as

q^{\mu}=(q^{+},q^{-},q_{\perp})=(\omega_{b},-\omega_{a},q_{\perp})

(4.104)

with
\omega_{a,b}>0
and expanded the
\delta
functions around these
\mathcal{O}(1)
components
\omega_{a,b}
.

From
section
4.1.2
, the LP current and its conjugate are given by

\displaystyle J^{\,\nu}(0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}}\sum_{f}(\gamma_{\perp}^{\nu})^{\alpha\beta}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,C^{(0)}(2{\tilde{p}}_{1}\cdot{\tilde{p}}_{2})\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},-\omega_{2}}^{\alpha\bar{a}}\bigl(S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\bigr)^{a\bar{b}}\chi_{{n_{1}},-\omega_{1}}^{\beta b}\,,

(4.105)

\displaystyle J^{\dagger\,\mu}(0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2}}\sum_{f^{\prime}}(\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu})^{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{2}\,C^{(0)}(2{\tilde{p}}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot{\tilde{p}}^{\prime}_{2})\bar{\chi}_{{n^{\prime}_{1}},\omega^{\prime}_{1}}^{\beta^{\prime}\bar{b}^{\prime}}\bigl(S_{n^{\prime}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n^{\prime}_{2}}\bigr)^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}}\chi_{n^{\prime}_{2},\omega^{\prime}_{2}}^{\alpha^{\prime}a^{\prime}}\,,

where for brevity we continue to suppress the flavor and explicit position of the fields at the origin.
In the following, fields and Wilson coefficients with prime momenta depend on
f^{\prime}
,
while the ones without primes depend on
f
, and ultimately will be fixed to equal,
f=f^{\prime}
.
Inserting eq. (
4.105
) together with eq. (
4.103
) into eq. (
4.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle W_{J^{(0)}}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n^{\prime}_{1},n^{\prime}_{2},n_{1},n_{2}}\sum_{f,f^{\prime}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}_{2}\,C^{(0)}(2{\tilde{p}}_{1}\cdot{\tilde{p}}_{2})C^{(0)}(2{\tilde{p}}^{\prime}_{1}\cdot{\tilde{p}}^{\prime}_{2})(\gamma_{\perp}^{\nu})^{\alpha\beta}(\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu})^{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\SumInt_{X_{n}}\SumInt_{X_{\bar{n}}}\SumInt_{X_{s}}\,\delta^{(2)}(\vec{q}_{T}+\vec{p}_{a,T}-\vec{p}_{b,T}-\vec{P}_{X_{s},T})

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigl\{\bigl[1+{\bar{n}}\cdot P_{X_{s}}\partial_{\omega_{a}}-n\cdot P_{X_{s}}\partial_{\omega_{b}}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\bigr]\,2\,\delta(\omega_{a}-{\bar{n}}\cdot p_{a})\,\delta(\omega_{b}-n\cdot p_{b})\bigr\}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Braket{N|\bar{\chi}_{{n^{\prime}_{1}},\omega^{\prime}_{1}}^{\beta^{\prime}\bar{b}^{\prime}}\bigl(S_{n^{\prime}_{1}}^{\dagger}S_{n^{\prime}_{2}}\bigr)^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}}\chi_{n^{\prime}_{2},\omega^{\prime}_{2}}^{\alpha^{\prime}a^{\prime}}|h,X_{n},X_{\bar{n}},X_{s}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Braket{h,X_{n},X_{\bar{n}},X_{s}|\bar{\chi}_{n_{2},-\omega_{2}}^{\alpha\bar{a}}\bigl(S_{n_{2}}^{\dagger}S_{n_{1}}\bigr)^{a\bar{b}}\chi_{{n_{1}},-\omega_{1}}^{\beta b}|N}\,.

(4.130)

Here, the directions
n_{i}
and momenta
\omega_{i}
are still arbitrary and thus summed and integrated over, respectively.
By employing that the hadron states are collinear and that
X
factorizes as in eq. (
4.100
),
we can factorize the matrix elements into their
n
-collinear,
{\bar{n}}
-collinear and soft sectors,
which then fixes these directions and momenta.
For example, one of the matrix elements we encounter is

\displaystyle\braket{N|\bar{\chi}_{{n^{\prime}_{1}},\omega^{\prime}_{1}}^{\beta^{\prime}\bar{b}^{\prime}}|X_{n}}\braket{X_{n}|\chi_{{n_{1}},-\omega_{1}}^{\beta b}|N}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ff^{\prime}}\delta_{n^{\prime}_{1},n}\delta_{n_{1},n}\theta(-\omega_{1})\delta(\omega^{\prime}_{1}-\omega_{1})

\displaystyle\quad\times\braket{N|\bar{\chi}_{n}^{\beta^{\prime}\bar{b}^{\prime}}|X_{n}}\braket{X_{n}|\chi_{n,-\omega_{1}}^{\beta b}|N}\,.

(4.131)

Here, we used that by flavor conservation the (suppressed) flavor indices must be equal,
and that the
n
-collinear nucleon state
N
fixes
n_{1}=n^{\prime}_{1}=n
.
Momentum conservation implies that both quark fields have equal momenta
\omega_{1}=\omega^{\prime}_{1}
,
such that we let the second quark field be unconstrained, and the
\theta
function
fixes the correct sign of the quark momentum following our conventions of
\chi_{n,-\omega}
.
Note that by fermion number conservation, we must have a
\bar{\chi}_{n}

and a
\chi_{n}
field in the
n
-collinear matrix element, and combinations
such as
\chi_{n}
and
\chi_{n}
are excluded.
Carrying out this straightforward algebra, we arrive at

\displaystyle W_{J^{(0)}}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f}\int\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\,|C^{(0)}(\omega_{1}\omega_{2})|^{2}(\gamma_{\perp}^{\nu})^{\alpha\beta}(\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu})^{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad\times\SumInt_{X_{n}}\SumInt_{X_{\bar{n}}}\SumInt_{X_{s}}\delta^{(2)}(\vec{q}_{T}+\vec{p}_{a,T}-\vec{p}_{b,T}-\vec{P}_{X_{s},T})

\displaystyle\quad\times\bigl\{\bigl[1+{\bar{n}}\cdot P_{X_{s}}\partial_{\omega_{a}}-n\cdot P_{X_{s}}\partial_{\omega_{b}}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\bigr]2\,\delta(\omega_{a}+\omega_{1})\,\delta(\omega_{b}-\omega_{2})\bigr\}

\displaystyle\quad\times\Bigl\{\theta(-\omega_{1})\braket{N|\bar{\chi}_{n}^{\beta^{\prime}\bar{b}^{\prime}}|X_{n}}\braket{X_{n}|\chi_{n,-\omega_{1}}^{\beta b}|N}\times\theta(\omega_{2})\braket{0|\chi_{{\bar{n}}}^{\alpha^{\prime}a^{\prime}}|h,X_{\bar{n}}}\braket{h,X_{\bar{n}}|\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}},-\omega_{2}}^{\alpha\bar{a}}|0}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\braket{0|\bigl(S_{n}^{\dagger}S_{{\bar{n}}}\bigr)^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}}|X_{s}}\braket{X_{s}|\bigl(S_{{\bar{n}}}^{\dagger}S_{n}\bigr)^{a\bar{b}}|0}

\displaystyle\qquad+\theta(-\omega_{1})\braket{N|\chi_{n}^{\alpha^{\prime}a^{\prime}}|X_{n}}\braket{X_{n}|\bar{\chi}_{n,-\omega_{1}}^{\alpha\bar{a}}|N}\times\theta(\omega_{2})\braket{0|\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}}^{\beta^{\prime}\bar{b}^{\prime}}|h,X_{\bar{n}}}\braket{h,X_{\bar{n}}|\chi_{{\bar{n}},-\omega_{2}}^{\beta b}|0}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\braket{0|\bigl(S_{{\bar{n}}}^{\dagger}S_{n}\bigr)^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}}|X_{s}}\braket{X_{s}|\bigl(S_{n}^{\dagger}S_{\bar{n}}\bigr)^{a\bar{b}}|0}\Bigr\}\,.

(4.156)

(Note that we have swapped
\omega_{1}\leftrightarrow\omega_{2}
labels for the antiquark contribution.)
Here, the two terms in the curly brackets encode the contributions from quarks
f
and antiquarks
\bar{f}
, respectively.
We have used that by momentum conservation
-\omega_{1}={\bar{n}}\cdot(P_{N}-P_{X_{n}})={\bar{n}}\cdot p_{a}

and
\omega_{2}=n\cdot(P_{h}+P_{X_{\bar{n}}})=n\cdot p_{b}
.
The derivatives only act on the
\delta
functions, and the sum over complete states is still pulled out
as the
\delta
functions act on the momenta of the
X_{i}
. To resolve this, we first rewrite

\displaystyle\delta^{(2)}({\vec{q}}_{T}+\vec{p}_{a,T}-\vec{p}_{b,T}-\vec{P}_{X_{s},T})

\displaystyle=\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{\vec{b}}_{T}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}{\vec{b}}_{T}\cdot({\vec{q}}_{T}-\vec{P}_{X_{n},T}-\vec{P}_{X_{\bar{n}},T}-\vec{P}_{X_{s},T})}\,,

(4.157)

where we used eq. (
4.102
) to set
\vec{p}_{a,T}=-\vec{P}_{X_{n},T}
and
\vec{p}_{b,T}=\vec{P}_{X_{\bar{n}},T}
.
We can then absorb the phases using the standard trick

\displaystyle e^{\mathrm{i}{\vec{b}}_{T}\cdot\vec{P}_{X_{n},T}}\braket{N|\bar{\chi}_{n}^{\beta^{\prime}\bar{b}^{\prime}}(0)|X_{n}}=\braket{N|\bar{\chi}_{n}^{\beta^{\prime}\bar{b}^{\prime}}(b_{\perp})|X_{n}}\,,

(4.158)

and likewise for the other matrix elements. For the soft matrix element, we also have to take care of
the
P_{X_{s}}^{\pm}
terms in the square brackets, which arise at NLP.
This can be achieved using the additional transformation

\displaystyle P_{X_{s}}^{+}\braket{0|(S_{n}^{\dagger}S_{{\bar{n}}})^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}}(0,0,b_{\perp})|X_{s}}

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{s}^{-}}e^{-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}b_{s}^{-}P_{X_{s}}^{+}}\braket{0|(S_{n}^{\dagger}S_{{\bar{n}}})^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}}(0,0,b_{\perp})|X_{s}}\Bigr|_{b_{s}^{-}=0}

\displaystyle=2\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{s}^{-}}\braket{0|(S_{n}^{\dagger}S_{{\bar{n}}})^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}}(0,b_{s}^{-},b_{\perp})|X_{s}}\Bigr|_{b_{s}^{-}=0}\,.

(4.159)

Here, the first line is a trivial equality, while in the second line we again used momentum conservation
to shift the soft current to the residual position
b_{s}^{-}
.
In this fashion, all dependence on the momenta
P_{X_{i}}
are expressed through either the labels
or the position of the quark fields and soft Wilson lines, and eq. (
4.2
) becomes

\displaystyle W_{J^{(0)}}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=(\gamma_{\perp}^{\nu})^{\alpha\beta}(\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu})^{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{\vec{b}}_{T}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}{\vec{b}}_{T}\cdot{\vec{q}}_{T}}\biggl[1+2\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{s}^{+}}\partial_{\omega_{a}}-2\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{s}^{-}}\partial_{\omega_{b}}+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2})\biggr]

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{f}2\mathcal{H}_{f}^{(0)}(-\omega_{a}\omega_{b})\Bigl[\hat{B}_{f/N}^{\beta\beta^{\prime}\,\bar{b}^{\prime}b}(\omega_{a},{\vec{b}}_{T})\mathcal{G}_{h/f}^{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha\,a^{\prime}\bar{a}}(\omega_{b},{\vec{b}}_{T})\hat{S}_{f}^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}a\bar{b}}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-})

\displaystyle\hskip 91.04872pt+\hat{B}_{\bar{f}/N}^{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha\,a^{\prime}\bar{a}}(\omega_{a},{\vec{b}}_{T})\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/\bar{f}}^{\beta\beta^{\prime}\,\bar{b}^{\prime}b}(\omega_{b},{\vec{b}}_{T})S_{\bar{f}}^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}a\bar{b}}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-})\Bigr]_{b_{s}^{\pm}=0}\,.

(4.160)

Here, we defined the LP hard function as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{f}^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})=|C_{f}^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})|^{2}\,,

(4.161)

where here
\tilde{q}^{2}=-\omega_{a}\omega_{b}=q^{+}q^{-}<0
.
The correlators for the quark contribution are given by

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{f/N}^{\beta\beta^{\prime}\,\bar{b}^{\prime}b}(\omega_{a},{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle=\theta(\omega_{a})\braket{N|\bar{\chi}_{n}^{\beta^{\prime}\bar{b}^{\prime}}(b_{\perp})\chi_{n,\omega_{a}}^{\beta b}(0)|N}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}^{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha\,a^{\prime}\bar{a}}(\omega_{b},{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle=\theta(\omega_{b})\SumInt_{X_{\bar{n}}}\braket{0|\chi_{{\bar{n}}}^{\alpha^{\prime}a^{\prime}}(b_{\perp})|h,X_{\bar{n}}}\braket{h,X_{\bar{n}}|\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}},-\omega_{b}}^{\alpha\bar{a}}|0}\,,

\displaystyle S_{f}^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}a\bar{b}}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-})

\displaystyle=\braket{0|\bigl(S_{n}^{\dagger}S_{{\bar{n}}}\bigr)^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}}(b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-},b_{\perp})\bigl(S_{{\bar{n}}}^{\dagger}S_{n}\bigr)^{a\bar{b}}(0)|0}\,,

(4.170)

and those for the antiquark contribution read

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{\bar{f}/N}^{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha\,a^{\prime}\bar{a}}(\omega_{a},{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle=\theta(\omega_{a})\braket{N|\chi_{n}^{\alpha^{\prime}a^{\prime}}(b_{\perp})\bar{\chi}_{n,\omega_{a}}^{\alpha\bar{a}}(0)|N}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/\bar{f}}^{\beta\beta^{\prime}\,\bar{b}^{\prime}b}(\omega_{b},{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle=\theta(\omega_{b})\SumInt_{X_{\bar{n}}}\braket{0|\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}}}^{\beta^{\prime}\bar{b}^{\prime}}(b_{\perp})|h,X_{\bar{n}}}\braket{h,X_{\bar{n}}|\chi_{{\bar{n}},-\omega_{b}}^{\beta b}(0)|0}\,,

\displaystyle S_{\bar{f}}^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}a\bar{b}}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-})

\displaystyle=\braket{0|\bigl(S_{{\bar{n}}}^{\dagger}S_{n}\bigr)^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}}(b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-},b_{\perp})\bigl(S_{n}^{\dagger}S_{\bar{n}}\bigr)^{a\bar{b}}(0)|0}\,.

(4.179)

In
\hat{B}_{i/N}
and
S_{f}
, we eliminated the sum over
X_{n}
and
X_{s}
using the completeness relation,
which is not possible for
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
,
unless we introduce creation and annihilation operators for the hadron
h
.
The Dirac indices on
\hat{B}_{i/N}
and
\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/i}
are chosen such that the index of the
\chi

field always comes before that of the
\bar{\chi}
field, which ensures that the Dirac indices in eq. (
4.2
) form a trace.
While we express the functions in terms of the Euclidean vector
{\vec{b}}_{T}
,
the fields are evaluated at position
b_{\perp}^{\mu}=(0,{\vec{b}}_{T},0)
.
In the case of the soft function, azimuthal symmetry implies that it only depends
on the magnitude
b_{T}\equiv|{\vec{b}}_{T}|
.
We also note that there is no time ordering in the soft function since the fields are spacelike separated and commute.

Next, we employ that the hadronic matrix elements are diagonal in color to define the color-traced objects

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{f/N}^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}(x=\omega_{a}/P_{N}^{-},{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle=\theta(\omega_{a})\,\braket{N|\bar{\chi}_{n}^{\beta^{\prime}}(b_{\perp})\chi_{n,\omega_{a}}^{\beta}(0)|N}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}^{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}(z=P_{h}^{+}/\omega_{b},{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta(\omega_{b})}{2zN_{c}}\SumInt_{X_{\bar{n}}}\operatorname{tr}\braket{0|\chi_{{\bar{n}}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}(b_{\perp})|h,X_{\bar{n}}}\braket{h,X_{\bar{n}}|\bar{\chi}_{{\bar{n}},-\omega_{b}}^{\alpha}|0}\,,

\displaystyle S(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{c}}\operatorname{tr}\braket{0|\bigl(S_{n}^{\dagger}S_{{\bar{n}}}\bigr)(b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-},b_{\perp})\bigl(S_{{\bar{n}}}^{\dagger}S_{n}\bigr)(0)|0}\,,

(4.188)

where the normalization factors are chosen as in the literature to ensure proper normalization at tree level.
We also defined the momentum fractions
x
and
z
for the collinear matrix elements.
Similar definitions arise for the antiquark distributions. Note that the soft function agrees
for quarks and antiquarks by symmetry under
n\leftrightarrow{\bar{n}}
, so we identify
S\equiv S_{f}=S_{\bar{f}}
. Although we are working in a general covariant gauge here, without explicit transverse Wilson lines at light-cone infinity, it is straightforward to generalize the steps above to include them, and we so in
appendix
C
, and give the full gauge invariant version of eq. (
4.2
) in eq. (
C
).

In eq. (
4.2
), the soft functions involve Wilson lines at position
b=(b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-},b_{\perp})
,
but by RPI-III invariance (longitudinal boosts) only depend on the combination
b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-}
.
The residual positions
b_{s}^{\pm}
are set to zero at the end, which at LP can be done immediately.
At NLP, it will allow us to evaluate the power corrections from the
\delta
function using derivatives w.r.t.
b_{s}^{\pm}
.
In the rest of this section, we focus on LP only, and will return to study the NLP terms in
section
4.5.3
.

Plugging this into eq. (
4.2
) and dropping the NLP terms,
we finally arrive at the LP hadronic structure function

\displaystyle W^{(0)\,\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=4z\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{\vec{b}}_{T}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}{\vec{b}}_{T}\cdot{\vec{q}}_{T}}\sum_{f}\mathcal{H}_{f}^{(0)}(q^{+}q^{-})\,\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[\hat{B}_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu}\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\gamma_{\perp}^{\nu}\Bigr]S(b_{T})

\displaystyle\quad+(f\to\bar{f}\,,\,\mu\leftrightarrow\nu)\,,

(4.189)

where
\mathrm{Tr}
traces over Dirac indices, and the
2z
compensates for the factor in eq. (
4.2
).
Here
S(b_{T})=S(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-}=0)
.
In the literature, it is common to absorb the soft function into the collinear matrix elements, which can be achieved as
14
14

14

For many rapidity regulators, one has that
B=\hat{B}\sqrt{S}=\hat{B}^{\rm(u)}/\sqrt{S}
, where
\hat{B}^{\rm(u)}
is calculated as an unsubtracted matrix element (ie. as a naive collinear matrix element without zero-bin subtractions
[
103
]
).
The precise equation here depends on the form of the zero-bin subtraction,
which often is equivalent to dividing by the full soft function
[
118
]
,
S^{\rm{z{-}b}}=S
,
such that
(\hat{B}^{\rm(u)}/S^{\rm{0bin}})\sqrt{S}=\hat{B}^{\rm(u)}/\sqrt{S}
.
Technically, for renormalized beam functions as in eq. (
5.50
),
one has to apply the zero-bin subtraction prior to renormalization,
explaining why
S^{\rm{0bin}}
is already subtracted in the definition of the renormalized
\hat{B}
.

\displaystyle B_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle\equiv\hat{B}_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\sqrt{S(b_{T})}\,,

(4.190)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle\equiv\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\sqrt{S(b_{T})}\,,

(4.191)

and similarly for
B_{\bar{f}/N}
and
\mathcal{G}_{h/\bar{f}}
.
Eq. (
4.2
) then becomes

\displaystyle W^{(0)\,\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=4z\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{\vec{b}}_{T}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}{\vec{b}}_{T}\cdot{\vec{q}}_{T}}\sum_{f}\mathcal{H}_{f}^{(0)}(q^{+}q^{-})\,\mathrm{Tr}\Bigl[B_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu}\mathcal{G}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\gamma_{\perp}^{\nu}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\quad+(f\to\bar{f}\,,\,\mu\leftrightarrow\nu)\,.

(4.192)

Here, the sum runs over quark flavors
f
, and the antiquark
\bar{f}
contribution
is added explicitly with flipped indices
\mu,\nu\to\nu,\mu
.
This only affects hadronic structure functions with antisymmetric dependence on
\mu\nu
,
which is however compensated by explicit sign change in the decomposition
of the corresponding antiquark correlators relative to the quark correlator,
such that the final structure functions will always be symmetric in
f\leftrightarrow\bar{f}
.

Note that eq. (
4.2
) holds with both bare and renormalized quantities, but is most useful in its renormalized form. Here we have suppressed dependence on the renormalization scale
\mu
, which will appear in renormalized expressions for
{\cal H}_{f}^{(0)}
,
B_{f/N}
, and
{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}
, as well as dependence on the Collins-Soper scales
\zeta_{a}=(xP_{N}^{-}e^{y_{n}})^{2}
in
B_{f/N}
and
\zeta_{b}=(P_{h}^{+}e^{-y_{n}}/z)^{2}
in
\mathcal{G}_{h/f}
.

4.3
Kinematic Corrections at NLP

The structure functions
W_{i}=P_{i}^{\mu\nu}W_{\mu\nu}
are defined by projecting
the hadronic tensor
W^{\mu\nu}
onto suitable projectors
P_{i}^{\mu\nu}
.
While the factorization of
W^{\mu\nu}
is derived in the factorization frame,
the
P_{i}^{\mu\nu}
are constructed in eq. (
2.2.1
) in terms of the unit vectors in the hadronic Breit frame.
As discussed in
section
2.3
, the unit vectors between the two frames
differ by
{\cal O}(\lambda)
corrections, which thus also affect the
W_{i}
themselves.
In this section, we discuss this source of
kinematic
power corrections.
To do so, it is easiest to expand the projectors the factorization frame up to next-to-leading power,

P_{i}^{\mu\nu}=P_{i}^{(0)\mu\nu}+P_{i}^{(1)\mu\nu}+\dots\,.

(4.193)

This can be achieved by plugging eq. (
2.3
) into eq. (
2.2.1
).
The leading power projectors
P_{i}^{(0)\mu\nu}
are simply
P_{i}^{\mu\nu}
with
\tilde{n}_{i}
replaced by
n_{i}
,
which gives rise to the LP structure functions
W_{i}^{(0)}=P_{i}^{(0)\mu\nu}W^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}
.
At NLP, one has to take power corrections from the hadronic tensor and the projector into account,
such that

\displaystyle W_{i}^{(1)}=P_{i}^{(1)\mu\nu}W^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}+P_{i}^{(0)\mu\nu}W^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}\,.

(4.194)

Since the first term here arises from the difference between the factorization and Breit frame,
we refer to this contributions as the kinematic power corrections. The second term
P_{i}^{(0)\mu\nu}W^{(1)}_{\mu\nu}

comprises the genuine NLP correction to the hadronic tensor and will be discussed in the sections below.

From eq. (
4.2
), we see that the Lorentz indices of
W^{(0)\,\mu\nu}
are purely transverse
in the factorization frame, i.e. transverse with respect to
n
and
{\bar{n}}
.
When contracting a projector with eq. (
4.2
), only the transverse part of the projector yields a nonzero contribution.
Eq. (
2.3
) implies that the
\tilde{n}_{x,y}
are purely transverse at leading power
and do not receive an additional transverse contribution at
\mathcal{O}(\lambda)
,
while
\tilde{n}_{t,z}
gain a transverse component at subleading power.
Since
\tilde{n}_{z}
does not contribute to any projector due to current conservation,
we conclude that only projectors containing one
\tilde{n}_{t}
induce kinematic power corrections at NLP, namely
P_{1,2,5,6}^{\mu\nu}
. We find

\displaystyle P_{1}^{(0)\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(n_{t}^{\mu}n_{x}^{\nu}+n_{x}^{\mu}n_{t}^{\nu})\,,\qquad\quad P_{1}^{(1)\mu\nu}=-\frac{q_{T}}{Q}n_{x}^{\mu}n_{x}^{\nu}+\dots\,,

\displaystyle P_{2}^{(0)\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(n_{t}^{\mu}n_{x}^{\nu}-n_{x}^{\mu}n_{t}^{\nu})\,,\qquad\quad P_{2}^{(1)\mu\nu}=\dots\,,

\displaystyle P_{5}^{(0)\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(n_{t}^{\mu}n_{y}^{\nu}+n_{y}^{\mu}n_{t}^{\nu})\,,\qquad\quad P_{5}^{(1)\mu\nu}=-\frac{q_{T}}{2Q}(n_{x}^{\mu}n_{y}^{\nu}+n_{y}^{\mu}n_{x}^{\nu})+\dots\,,

\displaystyle P_{6}^{(0)\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}(n_{y}^{\mu}n_{t}^{\nu}-n_{t}^{\mu}n_{y}^{\nu})\,,\qquad\quad P_{6}^{(1)\mu\nu}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}q_{T}}{2Q}(n_{y}^{\mu}n_{x}^{\nu}-n_{x}^{\mu}n_{y}^{\nu})+\dots\,.

(4.195)

Here
\dots
are terms proportional to
n^{\mu}
or
n^{\nu}
, which do not contribute when contracting with the leading power hadronic tensor.
Thus the structure functions
W_{1}^{X}
,
W_{2}^{X}
and
W_{6}^{X}
will receive kinematic power corrections. These kinematic power corrections are one of the nonzero contributions to the final NLP results given in
section
5
below.

4.4
J^{(0)}
with
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
Lagrangian Insertions at NLP

As our first potential non-trivial source of NLP corrections, lets continue to consider two leading power currents
J^{(0)\mu}
but now dress them with power suppressed insertions from the dynamic
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
Lagrangian, discussed in eq. (
3.14
). The relevant terms at NLP involve two insertions of
{\cal L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1/2)}
or one insertion of
{\cal L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1)}
. The resulting NLP corrections to the hadronic tensor are

\displaystyle W^{(1)\mu\nu}_{J^{(0)}\mathcal{L}}=

\displaystyle\SumInt_{X}\,\delta^{4}(q\!+\!P_{N}\!-\!P_{h}\!-\!P_{X})\bigg\{\!\int\!\!\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\Big[\braket{N|J^{(0)\dagger\,\mu}(0)|h,X}\braket{h,X|T\,J^{(0)\nu}(0)\mathcal{L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1)}(x)|N}

\displaystyle\quad+\braket{N|T\,J^{(0)\dagger\,\mu}(0)\mathcal{L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1)}(x)|h,X}\braket{h,X|J^{(0)\nu}(0)|N}\Big]

\displaystyle+\int\!\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\mathrm{d}^{4}y\,\Big[\braket{N|J^{(0)\dagger\,\mu}(0)|h,X}\braket{h,X|T\,J^{(0)\nu}(0)\mathcal{L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1/2)}(x)\mathcal{L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1/2)}(y)|N}

\displaystyle\quad+\braket{N|T\,J^{(0)\dagger\,\mu}(0)\mathcal{L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1/2)}(x)|h,X}\braket{h,X|T\,J^{(0)\nu}(0)\mathcal{L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1/2)}(y)|N}

\displaystyle\quad+\braket{N|T\,J^{(0)\dagger\,\mu}(0)\mathcal{L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1/2)}(x)\mathcal{L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1/2)}(y)|h,X}\braket{h,X|J^{(0)\nu}(0)|N}\Big]\bigg\}\,.

(4.204)

The terms we are interested in calculating at NLP are the structure functions that first obtain non-zero contributions at this order, which are
W_{1}^{X}
,
W_{2}^{X}
,
W_{5}^{X}
,
W_{6}^{X}
for various polarizations
X
, see
section
2.2
. To compute the NLP contribution to these structure functions we contract the leading power projectors
P_{i\mu\nu}^{(0)}
from eq. (
4.3
) with this subleading power hadronic tensor
W^{(1)\mu\nu}_{\mathcal{L}}
from eq. (
4.4
) for
i=1,2,5,6
. However, from eq. (
4.3
) we see that for
i=1,2,5,6
at least one of the indices
\mu
or
\nu
is contracted by a
n_{t}
vector. For the leading power current, the indices
\mu
and
\nu
are purely transverse, with
n_{t}^{\mu}J_{\mu}^{(0)\dagger}=0=n_{t}^{\nu}J_{\nu}^{(0)}
, and hence all potential contributions explicitly vanish at NLP. We note that this would no longer be the case at NNLP.

Thus we conclude that there are no contributions from Lagrangian insertions taken with the leading power
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
current to the SIDIS structure functions that start at NLP.

4.5
Soft Contributions at NLP

In this section we consider the various sources of power suppressed soft operators that could give contributions at NLP. This includes

i)

contributions involving subleading power hard Lagrangians
{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(i)}
which are obtained through the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
time ordered products

T\big[{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(0)}{\cal L}_{\rm I}^{(1)}\big]
,

T\big[{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(0)}{\cal L}_{\rm I}^{(2)}\big]
,

T\big[{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(0)}{\cal L}_{\rm I}^{(1)}{\cal L}_{\rm I}^{(1)}\big]
,

ii)

operators from
section
4.1.5
involving a
\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})\mu}
, whose final form was given in eq. (
4.90
),

iii)

operators which involve a
{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot\partial_{s}
and
{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}^{(n_{i})}
. Such terms appeared in the subleading power currents, given by the RPI protected result in eq. (
4.45
), and from the higher order terms documented in
section
4.2
that arise from expanding the momentum conserving
\delta
-function in the LP factorization theorem. These two types of contributions are related, for reasons we will explain.

We consider each of these contributions in turn.

4.5.1
Hard-Collinear Terms obtained from
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
with
{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(0)\mu}

In general the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
time ordered products
T\big[{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(0)}{\cal L}_{\rm I}^{(1)}\big]
,

T\big[{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(0)}{\cal L}_{\rm I}^{(2)}\big]
,

T\big[{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(0)}{\cal L}_{\rm I}^{(1)}{\cal L}_{\rm I}^{(1)}\big]

will give rise to
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
hard scattering operators in
{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(1/2)}
and
{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(1)}
, which then will contribute to observables at NLP. In the squared amplitude this occurs either through the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}

{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(1/2)}
taken with
{\cal L}_{\rm dyn}^{(1/2)}
and a
{\cal L}_{\rm hard}^{(0)}
, or through one
{\cal L}_{\rm hc}^{(1)}
and one
{\cal L}_{\rm hard}^{(0)}
. However for the structure functions that we are interested in, which start at NLP, these contributions vanish for the same reason as the terms considered in
section
4.4
. Here it is the presence of two
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
leading power operators
{\cal O}_{\rm I}^{(0)}
that are common to all these contributions, and which carry transverse indices
\mu
and
\nu
. The transverse nature of these
\mu
and
\nu
indices in
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
is retained by the matching onto
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
.

Thus the resulting contributions again have vanishing contraction with the leading power projectors
P_{i}^{(0)\mu\nu}
for
i=1,2,5,6
, yielding no contribution at NLP. Non-zero contributions can occur at NNLP where this argument breaks down.

4.5.2
Operators involving a
\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})\mu}

In this section, we consider contributions from the
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}
currents involving
\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})\mu}
given in eq. (
4.90
). For these contributions it will be necessary to carry out a factorization of the collinear and soft matrix elements in order to assess these potential NLP contributions.

The
\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n_{i})\mu}
contributions enter through the current
J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)\mu}
given in eq. (
4.90
). Calculating the matrix elements of

J^{(0)\mu\dagger}J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)\nu}+J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)\mu\dagger}J^{(0)\nu}\,,

(4.205)

using the procedure similar to the LP section, we get the factorized formula for the
\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}
operator contribution

\displaystyle W_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4z}{Q}\sum_{f}\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}b_{T}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\vec{q}_{T}\cdot{\vec{b}}_{T}}\,\int\!\!\mathrm{d}\hat{b}_{s}\,C^{(0)}(\tilde{q}^{2})

(4.206)

\displaystyle\times\biggl\{

\displaystyle(n^{\nu}\!+\!{\bar{n}}^{\nu})\,C_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)}\big(\tilde{q}^{2},\hat{b}_{s}\big)\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{B}_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu}\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma_{\perp\rho}\right]\hat{S}_{1}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},Q\hat{b}_{s},Q\hat{b}_{s})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(n^{\mu}\!+\!{\bar{n}}^{\mu})\,C_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)*}\big(\tilde{q}^{2},\hat{b}_{s}\big)\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{B}_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma_{\perp\rho}\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma_{\perp}^{\nu}\right]\hat{S}_{2}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},Q\hat{b}_{s},Q\hat{b}_{s})\biggr\}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(f\to\bar{f},\mu\leftrightarrow\nu\,,C_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)}\leftrightarrow C_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)*})

Here, the soft matrix elements
\hat{S}_{1}^{\rho}
and
\hat{S}_{2}^{\rho}
are defined as
15
15

15

From direct calculations, the soft matrix elements for antiquark would be eq. (
4.207
) with
n
and
\bar{n}
switched. A minus sign from the fact that
({\tilde{p}}_{2}-{\tilde{p}}_{1})^{\mu}
in eq. (
4.1.3
) picks out opposite signs for quark and antiquark, is then canceled by the minus sign due to eqs. (
4.209
) and (
4.214
). Therefore we don’t need to define extra soft functions for antiquarks.

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{1}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\equiv\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{c}}\operatorname{tr}\,\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert\bigl[S^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{\perp})\,S_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})\bigr]\bigl[S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{-})\,S_{n}(b_{s}^{-})g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n)\rho}(b_{s}^{-})\bigr]\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle

(4.207)

\displaystyle\ +\frac{1}{N_{c}}\operatorname{tr}\,\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert\bigl[S^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{\perp})\,S_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})\bigr]\bigl[g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}})\rho}(b_{s}^{+})S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{+})\,S_{n}(b_{s}^{+})\bigr]\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{2}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\equiv\,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{c}}\operatorname{tr}\,\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert\bigl[g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n)\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{-})\,S^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{-})\,S_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{-})\bigr]\bigl[S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(0)\,S_{n}(0)\bigr]\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\ +\frac{1}{N_{c}}\operatorname{tr}\,\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert\bigl[S^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+})\,S_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+})\,g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}})\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+})\bigr]\bigl[S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(0)\,S_{n}(0)\bigr]\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle\,.

Using the fact that the vacuum is parity invariant we find

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{1}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{c}}\operatorname{tr}\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert P^{-1}PS^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{\perp})P^{-1}PS_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})P^{-1}PS^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{-})P^{-1}PS_{n}(b_{s}^{-})P^{-1}Pg\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n)\rho}(b_{s}^{-})P^{-1}P\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N_{c}}\operatorname{tr}\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert P^{-1}PS^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{\perp})P^{-1}PS_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})P^{-1}Pg\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}})\rho}(b_{s}^{+})P^{-1}PS^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{+})P^{-1}PS_{n}(b_{s}^{+})P^{-1}P\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{N_{c}}\operatorname{tr}\Bigl\langle\!0\Bigr\rvert S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(-b_{\perp}\!)S_{n}(-b_{\perp}\!)S^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{s}^{+})S_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{+})g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}})\rho}(b_{s}^{+})\!+\!S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(-b_{\perp}\!)S_{n}(-b_{\perp}\!)g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n)\rho}(b_{s}^{-})S^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{s}^{-})S_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{-})\Bigr\rvert 0\!\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{N_{c}}\operatorname{tr}\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert S^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+})S_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+})g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}})\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+})S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(0)S_{n}(0)

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt+g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n)\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{-})S^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{-})S_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{-})S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(0)S_{n}(0)\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=-\hat{S}_{2}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\,.

(4.208)

In the second equality we used that under parity there is an overall sign flip associated to the spatial index
\rho
, and that
n\leftrightarrow{\bar{n}}
and
b_{s}^{+}\leftrightarrow b_{s}^{-}
. In the third equality we did a cyclic reordering of the fields in the trace (since they are spacelike separated and hence commute), and then used translation invariance to shift all fields by
+b_{\perp}
.

We also note that from the definitions and our ability to make cyclic reorderings and translations, that we have

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{1}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\Big|_{n\leftrightarrow{\bar{n}}}=\hat{S}_{2}^{\rho}(-b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\,,\quad\hat{S}_{2}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\Big|_{n\leftrightarrow{\bar{n}}}=\hat{S}_{1}^{\rho}(-b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\,.

(4.209)

Next we consider how these soft matrix elements are constrained by charge conjugation. Recall that the gluon field transforms under charge conjugation as

\displaystyle A_{\mu}=A_{\mu}^{A}T^{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C}}{{\longrightarrow}}A_{\mu}^{A}(\bar{T})^{A}=-A_{\mu}^{A}(T^{A})^{T}=-A_{\mu}^{T}\,,

(4.210)

where the superscript
T
denotes the transpose.
Under this transformation, a soft Wilson line in the fundamental representation transforms as

\displaystyle S_{n}^{ab}(b;0,\infty)=

\displaystyle~\bar{P}\exp\Big[-ig\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\mathrm{d}s\>n\cdot A_{s}(b+ns)^{T}\Big]^{ab}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C}}{{\longrightarrow}}

\displaystyle~P\exp\Big[+ig\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\mathrm{d}s\>n\cdot A_{s}(b+ns)\Big]^{ba}=S_{n}^{\dagger ba}(b;0,\infty)\,.

(4.211)

Here,
ab
are the color indices of the Wilson line, and the transpose of the product of color matrices reverses the path ordering.
For the soft gluon building block field

\displaystyle g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n)\rho\,ab}=\big[S_{n}^{\dagger}iD_{s\perp}^{\rho}S_{n}\big]^{ab}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C}}{{\longrightarrow}}\big[S_{n}^{\dagger}(i\overleftarrow{D}_{s\perp}^{\rho})S_{n}\big]^{ba}=-g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n)\rho\,ba}\,,

(4.212)

exactly like the gluon field in eq. (
4.210
). Using the fact that the vacuum is invariant under charge conjugation
C
, we find that

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{1}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{c}}\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert C^{-1}CS^{\dagger ab}_{n}(b_{\perp})C^{-1}CS^{bc}_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})C^{-1}CS^{\dagger cd}_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{-})C^{-1}CS^{de}_{n}(b_{s}^{-})C^{-1}Cg\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n)\rho\,ea}(b_{s}^{-})C^{-1}C\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{N_{c}}\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert C^{-1}CS^{\dagger ab}_{n}(b_{\perp})C^{-1}CS^{bc}_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})C^{-1}Cg\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}})\rho\,cd}(b_{s}^{+})C^{-1}CS^{\dagger de}_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{+})C^{-1}CS^{ea}_{n}(b_{s}^{+})C^{-1}C\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{N_{c}}\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert S^{ba}_{n}(b_{\perp})S^{\dagger cb}_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})\big[S^{dc}_{\bar{n}}\,S^{\dagger ed}_{n}g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n)\rho\,ae}\big](b_{s}^{-})+S^{ba}_{n}(b_{\perp})S^{\dagger cb}_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})\big[g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}})\rho\,dc}S^{ed}_{\bar{n}}\,S^{\dagger ae}_{n}\big](b_{s}^{+})\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{N_{c}}\operatorname{tr}\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n)\rho}(b_{s}^{-})S^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{s}^{-})S_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{-})S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})S_{n}(b_{\perp})+S^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{s}^{+})S_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{+})g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}})\rho}(b_{s}^{+})S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})S_{n}(b_{\perp})\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{-1}{N_{c}}\operatorname{tr}\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})S_{n}(b_{\perp})g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{(n)\rho}(b_{s}^{-})S^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{s}^{-})S_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{-})+S^{\dagger}_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})S_{n}(b_{\perp})S^{\dagger}_{n}(b_{s}^{+})S_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{+})g\mathcal{B}_{s\perp}^{({\bar{n}})\rho}(b_{s}^{+})\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=-\hat{S}_{1}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\bigg|_{n\leftrightarrow{\bar{n}}}=-\hat{S}_{2}^{\rho}(-b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\,.

(4.213)

In the third equality we reordered the fields from the second equality into a trace (since they are spacelike separated and hence commute). To obtain the fourth equality we used the cyclic property of the trace.

Since the index
\rho
in
\hat{S}_{i}^{\rho}
is transverse we can decompose
\hat{S}_{i}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})
as

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{i}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})=b_{\perp}^{\rho}\,S_{i}^{\parallel}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})+\epsilon_{\perp}^{\rho\sigma}b_{\perp\sigma}\,S^{\top}_{i}\!(b_{T},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\,,\qquad i=1,\,2\,.

(4.214)

Here
S_{i}^{\parallel}
and
S_{i}^{\top}
are scalar functions and hence depend only on the magnitude
b_{T}=|{\vec{b}}_{T}|
. The parity relation in eq. (
4.5.2
) then implies

\displaystyle S_{1}^{\parallel}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})=-S_{2}^{\parallel}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\,,\qquad S_{1}^{\top}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})=-S_{2}^{\top}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\,.

(4.215)

On the other hand, the charge conjugation relation in eq. (
4.5.2
) gives

\displaystyle S_{1}^{\parallel}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})=+S_{2}^{\parallel}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\,,\qquad S_{1}^{\top}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})=+S_{2}^{\top}(b_{T},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})\,.

(4.216)

Taken together these are contradictory, and hence the soft matrix elements defined in eq. (
4.207
) must vanish

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{1}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})=0\,,\qquad\qquad\hat{S}_{2}^{\rho}(b_{\perp},b_{s}^{+},b_{s}^{-})=0\,.

(4.217)

We have carried out a cross check on this symmetry argument by verifying that the perturbative calculation of these soft matrix element vanishes at the integrand level at one-loop.
As a further cross-check we have also considered various two loop contributions that were easy to analyze, including the fully abelian
C_{F}^{2}
terms, and terms involving quark, gluon, or ghost vacuum polarization type graphs. Again these all vanish at the integrand level, giving us full confidence in the all orders in
\alpha_{s}
symmetry argument presented here.

Thus at NLP there is no contribution from the
J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)\mu}
current to any
W_{i}^{X}
in SIDIS. The arguments based on discrete symmetries presented here will no longer carry through at NNLP, where we expect contributions from
J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)\dagger\mu}J_{\mathcal{B}_{s}^{\perp}}^{(1)\nu}
for example.

The argument given above for the vanishing of the NLP soft functions, based on the application of parity, charge conjugation, and other symmetries on the vacuum matrix elements, actually works just as well to eliminate the
{\bar{n}}_{i}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}^{(n_{i})}
contributions from the current in eq. (
4.45
).
The sum of
{\bar{n}}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}^{(n)}
and
n\cdot{\cal B}_{s}^{({\bar{n}})}
terms appearing in eq. (
4.45
) is just like the sum of terms appearing in eq. (
4.90
), and it is straightforward to show that the same symmetry relations in eqs. (
4.209
) and (
4.5.2
)) apply, and a parity relation like in eq. (
4.5.2
) applies but without the extra multiplicative minus sign on the RHS. This again leads to contradictory equations, and vanishing soft function for this current.
Rather than spelling out this argument in detail, we give an alternate argument in the next section, whereby these operators are considered together with others, and again give a vanishing soft NLP contribution.

4.5.3
Operators involving a
n\cdot\partial_{s}
,
{\bar{n}}\cdot\partial_{s}
,
{\bar{n}}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}^{(n)}
, or
n\cdot{\cal B}_{s}^{({\bar{n}})}

In this section we consider NLP contributions to
W^{\mu\nu}
that come from either a single
{\bar{n}}\cdot\partial_{s}
,
n\cdot\partial_{s}
,
{\bar{n}}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}^{(n)}
, or
n\cdot{\cal B}_{s}^{({\bar{n}})}
. There are two places that we have such contributions appeared:

a)

in the RPI expansion leading to the operators in eq. (
4.45
), and

b)

from expanding the momentum conserving
\delta
-function in the analysis of the leading power factorization theorem in
section
4.2
, shown in eq. (
4.2
).

Both of these results had their hard coefficient functions determined by a derivative of the leading power Wilson coefficient,
\partial C^{(0)}/\partial\omega_{i}
. In fact, it turns out that the results in a) and b) give identical contributions, and are just two different ways of constructing the same contribution. In both cases we can follow the same steps for the factorization as carried out in
section
4.2
, but with a difference in one step of the procedure.

In approach a) we setup the SCET field theory with exact momentum conservation for momenta of all sizes, which in particular implies momentum conservation for both
{\cal O}(\lambda^{0})
large
p_{n}^{-}
and
p_{\bar{n}}^{+}
momenta, as well as for
{\cal O}(\lambda)
soft
p_{s}^{-}
and
p_{s}^{+}
momenta which appear as residual momenta in the
n
-collinear and
{\bar{n}}
-collinear fields respectively.
In this approach, when we carry out the leading power factorization analysis there is never an expansion of the type carried out in eq. (
4.103
), since instead the momentum conserving
\delta
-function is written as a product of distributions that implement momentum conservation at each of the scales. In this approach residual momentum conserving
\delta
-functions shift the coordinates of fields to account for the residual momentum flow. This arises because the formulation of SCET implements an exact power expansion, with terms that are always homogeneous in
\lambda
, without the need for re-expansion to obtain subleading terms. In this approach, only the operators in eq. (
4.45
) give contributions of the type considered in this section.

In approach b) we instead expand the full momentum conserving
\delta
-function in powers of
\lambda
, as in eq. (
4.103
). In this approach the resulting collinear fields are already evaluated with spacetime arguments that force them to carry zero residual momenta. So for example an
n
-collinear field has a
p_{n}^{-}
momenta of
{\cal O}(\lambda^{0})
, but no residual minus-momentum of
{\cal O}(\lambda)
. Therefore, in this approach the operators in eq. (
4.45
) give zero contribution, either explicitly because the
n_{1}\cdot\partial_{s}
and
{\bar{n}}\cdot\partial_{s}
derivatives vanish, or due to momentum conservation, since no object in the factorization can balance the soft momentum carried by the one or more soft gluons emitted by the operator
\mathcal{O}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\tiny$\!\!\!(-)$}}}{{n_{1}}}\cdot{\cal B}_{s}}^{(1)\mu}
in eq. (
4.46
). Thus, in this approach only the NLP terms in eq. (
4.2
) give contributions.

To see that the two methods give exactly the same contribution, we can start with eq. (
4.2
). Combining eq. (
4.2
) with the rearrangement of color indices that leads to eq. (
4.2
), we see that the result for the NLP contribution involves either a
\mathrm{i}{\bar{n}}\cdot\partial_{s}=2\mathrm{i}\partial/\partial b_{s}^{+}
or a
\mathrm{i}n\cdot\partial_{s}=2\mathrm{i}\partial/\partial b_{s}^{-}
derivative of the leading power soft function
S(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-})
, after which we must send
b_{s}^{\pm}\to 0
. Looking inside this soft function, we see that the derivative acts on the Wilson lines as in eq. (
4.2
)

\displaystyle in\cdot\partial_{s}\big(S_{n}^{\dagger}S_{\bar{n}}\big)(b_{s}^{+})

\displaystyle=S_{n}^{\dagger}(b_{s}^{+})\Big\{in\cdot\overleftarrow{\partial}_{s}+in\cdot\partial_{s}\Big\}S_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{+})

\displaystyle=S_{n}^{\dagger}(b_{s}^{+})\Big\{in\cdot\overleftarrow{D}_{s}+in\cdot D_{s}\Big\}S_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{+})

\displaystyle=S_{n}^{\dagger}(b_{s}^{+})\,in\cdot D_{s}\,S_{\bar{n}}(b_{s}^{+})\,,

(4.218)

where in the last line we used the Wilson line equation of motion,
in\cdot D_{s}S_{n}=0
. The final operator in eq. (
4.5.3
) is the same as the one which led to the
in\cdot\partial_{s}
and
gn\cdot\mathcal{B}_{s}^{({\bar{n}})}
operators in eq. (
4.46
) through the use of eq. (
4.41
) (taken here with
n\leftrightarrow{\bar{n}}
). The same steps apply for the
i{\bar{n}}\cdot\partial_{s}(S_{n}^{\dagger}S_{\bar{n}})
term in eq. (
4.2
), where this time the covariant derivative
i{\bar{n}}\cdot D_{s}S_{\bar{n}}=0
, and this leads to the
i{\bar{n}}\cdot\partial_{s}
and
g{\bar{n}}\cdot\mathcal{B}_{s}^{(n)}
terms appearing in eq. (
4.46
). Thus the two approaches are equivalent and lead to the same result.

Adopting the already quite compact result from approach b),
we denote the NLP term from eq. (
4.2
) as
W^{(1)\mu\nu}_{\delta}
, and see that it corresponds to

\displaystyle W^{(1)\mu\nu}_{\delta}

\displaystyle=2\left(\mathrm{i}{\bar{n}}\cdot\partial_{s}\partial_{\omega_{a}}-\mathrm{i}n\cdot\partial_{s}\,\partial_{\omega_{b}}\right)\mathcal{H}^{(0)}_{f}(\omega_{a}\omega_{b})\,S(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-})

(4.219)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\biggl\{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{B}_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu}\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma_{\perp}^{\nu}\right]+(f\to\bar{f},\,\mu\leftrightarrow\nu)\left.\biggr\}\right|_{b_{s}^{+}=b_{s}^{-}=0}\,.

It is interesting to note that in this subleading TMD factorization formula that the
\omega_{a,b}
derivatives will hit both the hard function and the collinear functions (in contrast to
{\rm SCET}_{\rm I}
, where the analogous contributions typically only act on the hard function). Nevertheless, here
W_{\delta}^{(1)\mu\nu}
actually evaluates to zero due to the action of the derivatives and limits of the generalized soft function
S(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-})
. Essentially, this occurs because if we consider the possible form of results for

\displaystyle S_{\delta}^{-}(b_{T})=2\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{s}^{+}}S(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-})\bigg|_{b_{s}^{\pm}\to 0}\,,\qquad S_{\delta}^{+}(b_{T})=2\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{s}^{-}}S(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-})\bigg|_{b_{s}^{\pm}\to 0}\,,

(4.220)

then we must obtain a result for
S_{\delta}^{-}(b_{T})
that scales linear in
{\bar{n}}
(ie. as an inverse plus variable) under an RPI-III transformation, and for
S_{\delta}^{+}(b_{T})
that scales linear in
n
. However, there are no available quantities left to carry these scaling properties after we set
b_{s}^{\pm}=0
, thus the functions
S_{\delta}^{\pm}(b_{T})=0
.

To make the argument technically sound we have to consider the fact that the soft function has rapidity divergences associated to its dependence on light-cone positions in the limit
b_{s}^{\pm}\to 0
.
In particular, analytic perturbative results for the unregulated fully differential soft function
S(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-})
can be found in Refs.
[
31
,
37
]
, and can easily be used to confirm that the functions in eq. (
4.220
) exhibit power law divergences in the desired limit.
Therefore we must confirm that the above argument still applies in the presence of one or more of the possible rapidity regulators that are used for leading (and subleading) power TMDs in the literature
[
27
,
28
,
119
,
29
,
120
,
30
,
31
,
43
]
.
We anticipate that the vanishing result will still be achieved for any reasonable rapidity regulator.
16
16

16

To achieve a non-zero result the rapidity regulator would need to cause a hard breaking of rapidity scaling laws like a hard cutoff, as opposed to a soft breaking like those that mimic dimensional regularization for invariant-mass singularities. Even in the hard cutoff case it becomes clear that a non-zero result would be a regulator artifact, rather than a required contribution.
For illustration it is useful to choose a regulator that does not modify the fully-differential soft function
S(b_{T},b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-})
when it is removed in cases where
b_{s}^{+}b_{s}^{-}\neq 0
, and one that gives a nonvanishing soft function
S(b_{T})=S(b_{T},0)
at LP, so that it is clear that the regulator is not simply shifting the question we are aiming to answer into the collinear sector.
We will use the
\eta
regulator of refs.
[
120
,
30
]
, which is known to correctly regulate Glauber interactions in
{\rm SCET}_{\rm II}

[
97
]
,
and which has already been studied at subleading power for the
q_{T}
distribution in ref.
[
43
]
.
17
17

17

An important detail is that the
\eta
regulator acts on the
group momentum

k_{g}
,
which is defined as the total momentum flowing into a connected web (CWEB), see appendix A in ref.
[
30
]
. This is required in order to not spoil non-abelian exponentiation for Wilson line operators
[
121
,
122
]
.
It is the sum of these CWEBs that appear in the logarithm of the LP soft function, and which have only single primitive rapidity divergences,
\propto 1/\eta
, regardless of the order considered in perturbation theory. At subleading power the form of non-abelian exponentiation is also known for Wilson line operators
[
123
]
.

In momentum space the primitive regulated integral which corresponds to
S_{\delta}^{\pm}
are given by an integral over a (connected web) group momentum
k
, where the
\partial/\partial b_{s}^{\mp}
derivatives produce an extra
k^{+}
or
k^{-}
in the integrand:

\displaystyle I_{\delta}^{\pm}(k_{T})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!\mathrm{d}k^{+}\mathrm{d}k^{-}\>k^{\pm}\>I(k^{+}k^{-},k_{T}^{2},\epsilon)\>|k^{+}-k^{-}|^{-\eta}\nu^{\eta}

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}s\,s\,I(s,k_{T}^{2},\epsilon)\Big|\frac{\nu}{\sqrt{s}}\Big|^{-\eta}\int\mathrm{d}y\,e^{\mp y}\,|e^{y}-e^{-y}|^{-\eta}\,.

(4.221)

Here
I(k^{+}k^{-},k_{T}^{2},\epsilon)
is the RPI-III invariant LP integrand for the soft contribution at whatever order in
\alpha_{s}
is being considered, and contains within it all integrals other than that over the group momentum. It is defined to also includes dependence on the dimensional regularization parameter
\epsilon=(d-4)/2
and
\mu^{\epsilon}
factors. In addition
\nu
is a renormalization scale associated with rapidity divergences, and results are to be considered as expanded in the limit that the rapidity regulator is removed, as
\eta\to 0
.
In the second line of eq. (
4.5.3
) we have made a change of variable to an invariant mass variable
s=k^{+}k^{-}
and rapidity
y=\frac{1}{2}\ln(k^{-}/k^{+})
, noting that
dk^{+}dk^{-}=dsdy
. The fact that dimensional regularization does not suffice to regulate rapidity divergences is manifest in eq. (
4.5.3
) due to the fact that the
\epsilon
dependence does not influence the
y
integral. In general the rapidity integral

\displaystyle I_{\eta}^{(\alpha)}(s/\nu^{2})

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\frac{\nu^{2}}{s}\Bigr)^{\eta/2}\int\mathrm{d}y\,e^{\alpha y}\,|e^{y}-e^{-y}|^{-\eta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\Bigl(\frac{\nu^{2}}{s}\Bigr)^{\eta/2}\cos\Bigl(\frac{\alpha\pi}{2}\Bigr)\sin\Bigl(\frac{\eta\pi}{2}\Bigr)\Gamma(1-\eta)\Gamma\Bigl(\frac{\eta-\alpha}{2}\Bigr)\Gamma\Bigl(\frac{\eta+\alpha}{2}\Bigr)\,,

(4.222)

which is obtained by the appropriate analytic
continuation in
\eta

[
43
]
. The remaining integration over
s
in eq. (
4.5.3
) does not add any additional
1/\eta
divergences. This is the case because the
s
integral is regulated by
\epsilon
, and one must consider
\eta\to 0
for fixed
\epsilon
so that rapidity divergences are handled independent from invariant mass divergences
[
30
]
.
Expanding eq. (
4.5.3
) in
\eta\to 0
, we obtain
I_{\eta}^{(\alpha)}(s/\nu^{2})=0
for all odd
\alpha
, and in particular for the integral that appears in eq. (
4.5.3
) for the NLP result,
I_{\eta}^{(\pm 1)}(s/\nu^{2})=0
. This vanishing of the NLP terms was also discussed explicitly for Drell-Yan at NLO in ref.
[
43
]
.
In contrast, the integral that appears at leading power is
I_{\eta}^{(0)}(s/\nu^{2})\neq 0
and this regulator gives a non-trivial leading power soft function. We refer the reader to ref.
[
43
]
for a more detailed discussion of regulating rapidity divergences at subleading power, including effects at NNLP which can generically be non-zero, but which also must be considered hand in hand with collinear effects at that order.

Thus at NLP, i.e.
\alpha=\pm 1
, the soft functions vanish analytically even in the presence of a rapidity regulator,
S_{\delta}^{\pm}(k_{T})=0
,
and hence
W_{\delta}^{(1)\mu\nu}=0
, and does not contribute to the SIDIS factorization formula at this order.

4.6
NLP Contributions from the
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
Operators

We next consider contributions from the operators with a
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
insertion, given in eq. (
4.39
).
Since these operators have the same field content and Wilson coefficient as the leading-power currents,
calculating their contribution to the hadronic tensor proceeds analogous to the LP case discussed in
section
4.2
.
Schematically, one inserts the currents

\Bigl(J_{\mathcal{P}}^{(1)\mu\dagger}+J_{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}}^{(1)\mu\dagger}\Bigr)J^{(0)\nu}\quad{\rm and}\quad J^{(0)\mu\dagger}\Bigl(J_{\mathcal{P}}^{(1)\nu}+J_{\mathcal{P}^{\dagger}}^{(1)\nu}\Bigr)

(4.223)

into eq. (
4
). The explicit derivation parallels that in
section
4.2
,
so we only discuss the final result,

\displaystyle\hat{W}^{(1)\mu\nu}_{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle=4z\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{\vec{b}}_{T}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-\mathrm{i}{\vec{b}}_{T}\cdot{\vec{q}}_{T}}\sum_{f}\,\mathcal{H}^{(0)}_{f}(q^{+}q^{-})\,S(b_{T})

(4.224)

\displaystyle\times\Big\{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{B}_{\mathcal{P}\,f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma^{\nu}+\hat{B}_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}\,h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+(f\to\bar{f},\,\mu\leftrightarrow\nu)\Big\}\,.

Here,
\hat{B}_{f/N}
and
\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}
are the LP correlators defined in eq. (
4.2
),
and the new correlators involving the
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
operator are defined as

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{\mathcal{P}\,f/N}^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\theta(\omega_{a})}{2Q}\biggl\{\Bigl\langle N\Bigr\rvert\bar{\chi}_{n}^{\beta^{\prime}}(b_{\perp})\left[\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}\not{{\bar{n}}}\,\chi_{n,\omega_{a}}(0)\right]^{\beta}\Bigr\rvert N\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 51.21504pt+\Bigl\langle N\Bigr\rvert\left[\bar{\chi}_{n}(b_{\perp})\,\not{{\bar{n}}}\,\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}^{\dagger}\right]^{\beta^{\prime}}\,\chi_{n,\omega_{a}}^{\beta}(0)\Bigr\rvert N\Bigr\rangle\biggr\}

(4.225a)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\mathrm{i}}{2Q}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\perp}^{\rho}}\left[\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\,\not{{\bar{n}}}\,,\,\hat{B}_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\right]^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,.

(4.225b)

and

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}\,h/f}^{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{\theta(\omega_{b})}{4zN_{c}Q}\SumInt_{X_{\bar{n}}}\biggl\{\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert\left[\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}\not{n}\,\chi_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})\right]^{\alpha^{\prime}}\Bigr\rvert h,X_{\bar{n}}\Bigr\rangle\Bigl\langle h,X_{\bar{n}}\Bigr\rvert\bar{\chi}_{\bar{n},-\omega_{b}}^{\alpha}(0)\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert\chi_{\bar{n}}^{\alpha^{\prime}}(b_{\perp})\Bigr\rvert h,X_{\bar{n}}\Bigr\rangle\Bigl\langle h,X_{\bar{n}}\Bigr\rvert\left[\bar{\chi}_{\bar{n},-\omega_{b}}(0)\,\not{n}\,\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}^{\dagger}\right]^{\alpha}\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle\biggr\}

(4.226i)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2Q}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\perp}^{\rho}}\left[\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\,\not{n}\,,\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\right]^{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,.

(4.226j)

In eqs. (
4.225b
) and (
4.226j
), we used that
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{\rho}

can be expressed as
\mathrm{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\perp\rho}}
in position space.
The corresponding antiquark correlators are defined as

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{\mathcal{P}\,\bar{f}/N}^{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{-\theta(\omega_{a})}{2Q}\biggl\{\Bigl\langle N\Bigr\rvert\chi_{n}^{\alpha^{\prime}}(b_{\perp})\,\left[\bar{\chi}_{n,\omega_{a}}(0)\,\not{{\bar{n}}}\,\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}^{\dagger}\right]^{\alpha}\Bigr\rvert N\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 51.21504pt+\Bigl\langle N\Bigr\rvert\left[\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}\,\not{{\bar{n}}}\,\chi_{n}(b_{\perp})\right]^{\alpha^{\prime}}\,\chi_{n,\omega_{a}}^{\alpha}(0)\Bigr\rvert N\Bigr\rangle\biggr\}

(4.227a)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\mathrm{i}}{2Q}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\perp}^{\rho}}\left[\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\,\not{{\bar{n}}}\,,\,\hat{B}_{\bar{f}/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\right]^{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha}\,,

(4.227b)

and

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}\,h/\bar{f}}^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{-\theta(\omega_{b})}{4zN_{c}Q}\SumInt_{X_{\bar{n}}}\biggl\{\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert\left[\bar{\chi}_{\bar{n}}(b_{\perp})\,\not{n}\,\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}^{\dagger}\right]^{\beta^{\prime}}\Bigr\rvert h,X_{\bar{n}}\Bigr\rangle\Bigl\langle h,X_{\bar{n}}\Bigr\rvert\chi_{\bar{n},-\omega_{b}}^{\beta}(0)\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\Bigl\langle 0\Bigr\rvert\bar{\chi}_{\bar{n}}^{\beta^{\prime}}(b_{\perp})\Bigr\rvert h,X_{\bar{n}}\Bigr\rangle\Bigl\langle h,X_{\bar{n}}\Bigr\rvert\left[\not{\mathcal{P}}_{\perp}\not{n}\,\chi_{\bar{n},-\omega_{b}}(0)\right]^{\beta}\Bigr\rvert 0\Bigr\rangle\biggr\}

(4.228i)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2Q}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\perp}^{\rho}}\left[\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\,\not{n}\,,\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/\bar{f}}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\right]^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}\,.

(4.228j)

Notice that the sign difference between eqs. (
4.225
) and (
4.227
) arises
because the prefactors
1/(2\omega_{1})
and
1/(2\omega_{2})
in eq. (
4.40
)
pick different signs in the quark and antiquark contributions.
There is no such sign difference in eqs. (
4.225b
) and (
4.227b
)
because
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}^{\dagger}
act on the opposite fields
when going from eq. (
4.225
) to eq. (
4.227
), which results
in an additional minus sign.

In eq. (
4.224
), we have not absorbed the soft function into the hadronic matrix elements.
To do so, we define

\displaystyle W^{(1)\mu\nu}_{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle=4z\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{\vec{b}}_{T}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-\mathrm{i}{\vec{b}}_{T}\cdot{\vec{q}}_{T}}\sum_{f}\mathcal{H}^{(0)}_{f}(q^{+}q^{-})

(4.229)

\displaystyle\ \times\Big\{\mathrm{Tr}\left[B_{\mathcal{P}\,f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\mathcal{G}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma^{\nu}+B_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}\,h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma^{\nu}\right]

\displaystyle\ \quad+(f\to\bar{f},\,\mu\leftrightarrow\nu)\Big\}.

The soft-subtracted correlators are defined analogous to
eqs. (
4.225b
) and (
4.226j
) as

\displaystyle B_{\mathcal{P}\,f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{-\mathrm{i}}{2Q}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\perp}^{\rho}}\left[\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\,\not{{\bar{n}}}\,,\,B_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\right]\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}\,h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2Q}\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\perp}^{\rho}}\left[\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\,\not{n}\,,\,\mathcal{G}_{h/f}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\right]\,.

(4.230)

Comparing eqs. (
4.225
) and (
4.6
),

\displaystyle B_{\mathcal{P}\,f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle=\hat{B}_{\mathcal{P}\,f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\sqrt{S(b_{T})}-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2Q}\left[\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\,\not{{\bar{n}}}\,,\,\hat{B}_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\right]\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\perp}^{\rho}}\sqrt{S(b_{T})}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}\,h/f}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{P}\,h/f}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\sqrt{S(b_{T})}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2Q}\left[\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\,\not{n}\,,\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\right]\frac{\partial}{\partial b_{\perp}^{\rho}}\sqrt{S(b_{T})}\,,

(4.231)

we see that one can not absorb the soft function in the same fashion as at LP
due to the additional derivative acting on
S(b_{T})
itself.
This implies that
\hat{W}^{(1)\mu\nu}_{\mathcal{P}}
and
W^{(1)\mu\nu}_{\mathcal{P}}

also differ by such terms. Fortunately, these turn out to be proportional
to either
n^{\mu}-{\bar{n}}^{\mu}=2n_{z}^{\mu}
or
n^{\nu}-{\bar{n}}^{\nu}=2n_{z}^{\nu}
,
whose contractions with the leading-power projectors

P^{(0)}_{\mu\nu}
vanish by current conservation.
Explicitly, we have

\displaystyle W^{(1)\mu\nu}_{\mathcal{P}}-\hat{W}^{(1)\mu\nu}_{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle=4z\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{\vec{b}}_{T}}{(2\pi)^{2}}e^{-\mathrm{i}{\vec{b}}_{T}\cdot{\vec{q}}_{T}}\sum_{f}\bigl[\mathcal{H}^{(0)}_{f}(q^{2})\Delta^{\mu\nu}_{\mathcal{P}\,f}+(f\to\bar{f},\,\mu\leftrightarrow\nu)\bigr]

(4.232)

where the difference of the trace terms evaluates to

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mu\nu}_{\mathcal{P}\,f}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\mathrm{i}}{4Q}\frac{\partial S(b_{T})}{\partial b_{\perp}^{\rho}}\biggl\{\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{B}_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\bigl(\gamma^{\nu}\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\not{{\bar{n}}}+\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\not{n}\,\gamma^{\nu}\bigr)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\qquad-\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{B}_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\bigl(\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\not{{\bar{n}}}\gamma^{\mu}+\gamma^{\mu}\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\not{n}\bigr)\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma^{\nu}\right]\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\frac{-\mathrm{i}}{2Q}\frac{\partial S(b_{T})}{\partial b_{\perp}^{\rho}}\biggl\{(n^{\nu}-{\bar{n}}^{\nu})\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{B}_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma^{\mu}\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\qquad+(n^{\mu}-{\bar{n}}^{\mu})\,\mathrm{Tr}\left[\hat{B}_{f/N}(x,{\vec{b}}_{T})\gamma_{\perp}^{\rho}\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}(z,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\gamma^{\nu}\right]\biggr\}\,.

(4.233)

Here, we combined the soft factors coming from
B_{\mathcal{P}\,f/N},\mathcal{G}_{h/f}
and
B_{f/N},\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{P}\,h/f}

as
(\partial_{b_{\perp}^{\rho}}\sqrt{S})\sqrt{S}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{b_{\perp}^{\rho}}S
,
and in the second step used that the LP correlators obey

\not{n}\hat{B}_{f/N}=\not{{\bar{n}}}\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}=0
.
In conclusion, we find that
W^{(1)\mu\nu}_{\mathcal{P}}
and
\hat{W}^{(1)\mu\nu}_{\mathcal{P}}
agree up
to subleading (i.e. at least NNLP) corrections.

Just as the soft function for the
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
operators was the same as at leading power, we also can anticipate that the soft subtractions will be the same with various choices of rapidity regulator. In the soft limit the interactions of the collinear fields in these subleading operators behave like those of the soft Wilson lines in the soft function, so the only difference for the soft subtraction calculation is (potentially) the appearance of a different rapidity regulator. (In the soft subtraction the rapidity regulator must be the same as that used in the unsubtracted collinear matrix elements, while with some choices of rapidity regulator it differs for the soft function itself.)
In our SCET matrix elements in eqs. (
4.225
–
4.228
), the soft subtractions are encoded as part of the collinear propagators, which act as distributions that induce the subtractions, see
[
103
]
. If the soft subtractions are written as distinct vacuum matrix elements, following the method in ref.
[
124
]
, then the same argument given above for
S
will enable us to commute this matrix element with the
\partial/\partial b_{\perp}^{\rho}
derivative.
18
18

18

The argument proceeds as follows. We consider the matrix element in eq. (
4.225
), but using instead collinear fields without restrictions (which include couplings to the zero-bin collinear gluons), giving a result which we label with an extra prime,
\hat{B}_{\mathcal{P}\,f/N}^{\prime\beta\beta^{\prime}}
. We then make field redefinitions to remove the zero-bin contributions and obtain the collinear fields that we used here
[
124
]
: one on the
n\cdot A_{n}^{\prime}
collinear gluons and a second one on the Wilson lines
W_{n}^{\prime}
. Since
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
is a linear operator, the same steps used to derive eq. (
4.225b
) go through, but now we have
\hat{B}_{f/N}^{\prime}=\hat{B}_{f/N}S^{\rm{0bin}}
as the argument in the second slot of the commutator. This
S^{\rm{0bin}}
is the same as subtraction as at leading power, and also the same for TMD PDFs and FFs. Therefore, the same argument leading to our eq. (
4.232
) implies that the
\partial/\partial b_{\perp}^{\rho}
derivative on
S^{\rm{0bin}}
can be dropped, implying that
S^{\rm{0bin}}
can be pulled outside of the commutator and derivative. Together with our result in eq. (
4.225
) this then implies

\hat{B}_{\mathcal{P}\,f/N}^{\beta\beta^{\prime}}=\hat{B}_{\mathcal{P}\,f/N}^{\prime\beta\beta^{\prime}}/S^{\rm{0bin}}
. Thus the soft subtractions take the same multiplicative form as at leading power, and can be grouped and manipulated along with the soft function.

Hence the subtractions do not change any of the manipulations above.

Thus, we have demonstrated the non-trivial fact that the full contributions from
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
operators can be entirely expressed in terms of the leading power TMDs, which absorb the leading power soft function, and also have the same leading power hard function. Our final result for
W_{\mathcal{P}}^{(1)\mu\nu}
is thus given by eq. (
4.229
) with eq. (
4.6
). We also remark, that although we have neglected transverse Wilson lines at light-cone infinity by working only in covariant gauges here, the generalization to fully account for these transverse gauge links in the
\mathcal{P}_{\perp}
operators is carried out in
appendix
C
, leading eqs. (
4.225
) and (
4.226
) to be modified, and giving the results in eqs. (
C
) and (
C
). It is shown there that the relations in eqs. (
4.225b
) and (
4.226j
) remain valid when transverse gauge links are included.

It is straightforward to use the projection relations
\not{n}\,\hat{B}=\not{{\bar{n}}}\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}=0
to show that eq. (
4.229
) is composed of terms with one Lorentz index transverse, and the other one proportional to
n
or
{\bar{n}}
(this point will also be clear from calculations in
section
5.2.2
).
Therefore, when contracting with the leading power projectors
P_{i\mu\nu}^{(0)}
(given by eq. (
2.2.1
) with
\tilde{n}_{x,y,t}\to n_{x,y,t}
), eq. (
4.229
) has contributions to the structure functions whose corresponding projectors have one transverse index and one
n_{t}
, namely
W_{1,2,5,6}^{X}
. In contrast, eq. (
4.229
) does not contribute to the structure functions
W_{-1,3,4,7}^{X}
, at NLP because their corresponding
P_{i\mu\nu}^{(0)}
projectors have two transverse indices.

4.7
NLP Contributions from the Collinear
\mathcal{B}_{n_{i}\perp}
Operators

It remains to consider contributions from the operators with an additional
\mathcal{B}_{n_{1}\perp}
or
\mathcal{B}_{{\bar{n}}_{1}\perp}
field,
given in eq. (
4.77
). As usual, we obtain the corresponding contribution to the hadronic tensor
by inserting the currents

\displaystyle\Bigl(J^{(1)\dagger\mu}_{\mathcal{B}1}+J^{(1)\dagger\mu}_{\mathcal{B}2}\Bigr)J^{(0)\nu}\quad{\rm and}\quad J^{(0)\dagger\mu}\Bigl(J^{(1)\nu}_{\mathcal{B}1}+J^{(1)\nu}_{\mathcal{B}2}\Bigr)

(4.234)

into eq. (
4
). We start by studying the contribution from
J^{(0)\mu\dagger}J^{(1)\nu}_{\mathcal{B}1}
,
for which the relevant currents are given in eqs. (
4.29
) and (
4.70a
).
Inserting these into eq. (
4
) and performing a similar algebra as in
section
4.2
,
we arrive at

\displaystyle W^{(1)\,\mu\nu}_{J^{(0)}J^{(1)}_{\mathcal{B}1}}

\displaystyle=\SumInt_{X}\,\delta^{4}(q+P_{N}-P_{h}-P_{X})\,\braket{N|J^{(0)\mu\dagger}(0)|h,X}\braket{h,X|J^{(1)\nu}_{\mathcal{B}1}(0)|N}

\displaystyle=-2(\gamma_{\perp}^{\mu})^{\beta^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}(\gamma_{\perp\rho})^{\alpha\beta}\,\sum_{f}\int\!\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{\vec{b}}_{T}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\,e^{-\mathrm{i}\vec{q}_{T}\cdot\vec{b}_{T}}\int\!\mathrm{d}\xi\,C^{(0)}(-\omega_{a}\omega_{b})\,C^{(1)}(-\omega_{a}\omega_{b},\,\xi)

\displaystyle\quad\times\biggl[\frac{\omega_{a}n^{\nu}+\omega_{b}\bar{n}^{\nu}}{\omega_{b}}\hat{B}_{\mathcal{B}\,f/N}^{\rho\,\beta\beta^{\prime}\,\bar{b}^{\prime}b}(\omega_{a},\xi,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{h/f}^{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha\,a^{\prime}\bar{a}}(\omega_{b},{\vec{b}}_{T})\,S_{f}^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}a\bar{b}}(b_{T})

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\omega_{a}n^{\nu}+\omega_{b}\bar{n}^{\nu}}{\omega_{a}}\hat{B}_{\bar{f}/N}^{\alpha^{\prime}\alpha\,a^{\prime}\bar{a}}(\omega_{a},{\vec{b}}_{T})\,\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{B}\,h/\bar{f}}^{\dagger\rho\,\beta\beta^{\prime}\,\bar{b}^{\prime}b}(\omega_{b},\xi,{\vec{b}}_{T})\,S_{\bar{f}}^{b^{\prime}\bar{a}^{\prime}a\bar{b}}(b_{T})\biggr]\,.

(4.243)

Here, the first term in square brackets is the quark contribution,
while the second term is the antiquark contribution.
The relative sign between these terms arises because the kinematic prefactor

({\tilde{p}}_{1}^{\nu}-{\tilde{p}}_{2}^{\nu}+{\tilde{p}}_{3}^{\nu})
in the Wilson coefficient, eq. (
4.71
),
has opposite sign for the two contributions. Since there is only a single
subleading operator insertion, each of the two terms in eq. (
4.7
)
contains two leading-power correlators, as defined in
eqs. (
4.2
) and (
4.2
),
as well as a single NLP quark-gluon-quark correlator. We define the latter as

\displaystyle\hat{B}_{\mathcal{B}\,f/N}^{\rho\,\beta\beta^{\prime}\,\bar{b}^{\prime}b}(\omega_{a},\xi,{\vec{b}}_{T})

\displaystyle=\theta(\omega_{a})\Bigl\langle N\Bigr\rvert\bar{\chi}_{n}^{\beta^{\prime}\bar{b}^{\prime}}(b_{\perp})\,g{\mathcal{B}}_{n\perp,\,-\xi\omega_{a}}^{\rho\,b\bar{c}}(0)\,\chi_{n,\,(1-\xi)\omega_{a}}^{\beta c}(0)\Bigr\rvert N\Bigr\rangle\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathcal{B}\,h/\bar{f}}^{\dagger\,\rho\,\beta\beta^{\prime}\,\bar{b}^{\prime}b}(\omega_{b},\xi,{\vec{b}}_{T})
